### Predictability Shapes Adaptation: An Evolutionary Perspective on Modes of Learning in Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09855v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Y. Ku, Thomas L. Griffiths, Stephanie C. Y. Chan
- **Abstract**: Transformer models learn in two distinct modes: in-weights learning (IWL), encoding knowledge into model weights, and in-context learning (ICL), adapting flexibly to context without weight modification. To better understand the interplay between these learning modes, we draw inspiration from evolutionary biology's analogous adaptive strategies: genetic encoding (akin to IWL, adapting over generations and fixed within an individual's lifetime) and phenotypic plasticity (akin to ICL, enabling flexible behavioral responses to environmental cues). In evolutionary biology, environmental predictability dictates the balance between these strategies: stability favors genetic encoding, while reliable predictive cues promote phenotypic plasticity. We experimentally operationalize these dimensions of predictability and systematically investigate their influence on the ICL/IWL balance in Transformers. Using regression and classification tasks, we show that high environmental stability decisively favors IWL, as predicted, with a sharp transition at maximal stability. Conversely, high cue reliability enhances ICL efficacy, particularly when stability is low. Furthermore, learning dynamics reveal task-contingent temporal evolution: while a canonical ICL-to-IWL shift occurs in some settings (e.g., classification with many classes), we demonstrate that scenarios with easier IWL (e.g., fewer classes) or slower ICL acquisition (e.g., regression) can exhibit an initial IWL phase later yielding to ICL dominance. These findings support a relative-cost hypothesis for explaining these learning mode transitions, establishing predictability as a critical factor governing adaptive strategies in Transformers, and offering novel insights for understanding ICL and guiding training methodologies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the interplay between in-weights learning (IWL) and in-context learning (ICL) in Transformer models, drawing an analogy to evolutionary biology's genetic encoding and phenotypic plasticity.  It posits that environmental predictability (stability and cue reliability) shapes the balance between ICL and IWL.  Through controlled experiments on sinusoid regression and Omniglot classification tasks, the authors demonstrate that high environmental stability favors IWL, while high cue reliability enhances ICL.  They also show that learning dynamics can be task-dependent, exhibiting both ICL-to-IWL and IWL-to-ICL transitions. A relative-cost hypothesis is presented to explain these dynamics, suggesting that the computational cost of acquiring ICL versus IWL solutions influences the learning mode preference.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novel Analogy:** The paper's central strength lies in its innovative application of evolutionary biology principles to understand learning dynamics in Transformers. This analogy provides a fresh perspective on the ICL/IWL relationship, offering valuable insights beyond purely technical explanations.
    * **Controlled Experiments:** The experimental design is well-structured, allowing for systematic manipulation of key variables (environmental stability and cue reliability) and clear observation of their effects.  The use of both regression and classification tasks adds to the robustness of the findings.
    * **Clear and Articulate Explanation:** The paper clearly explains complex concepts, defines terminology rigorously, and presents results in a visually appealing and easily understandable format.
    * **Addresses an Important Open Question:** The paper tackles a critical open question in the field of deep learning: understanding the mechanisms driving ICL and its interaction with traditional weight-based learning.
    * **Relative Cost Hypothesis:** Offers a compelling hypothesis to explain task-dependent transience.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Simplified Tasks:** The reliance on sinusoid regression and Omniglot classification, while enabling controlled experimentation, limits the direct applicability of the findings to real-world, complex natural language tasks. It is not obvious that the dynamics would generalize to more complex scenarios.
    * **Computational Cost Interpretation:** While the relative-cost hypothesis is intriguing, the precise mechanisms and factors contributing to the computational cost of ICL versus IWL in Transformers are not fully elucidated. Further investigation into the underlying architectural elements that contribute to these costs would strengthen this argument. The paper provides empirical evidence for how varying the task difficulty affects the relative performance of ICL/IWL, but does not directly measure the computational cost.
    * **Lack of Mechanistic Explanation:** The paper focuses on a computational-level explanation, as described by Marr's levels. The paper lacks a mechanistic description, and while it uses analogy for intuition, the underlying mechanisms of ICL/IWL are not thoroughly explained from that level.
    * **Limited novelty:** While interesting, the paper does not add much to what is currently available in literature.

* **Significance and Potential Impact:**
    * The paper's evolutionary perspective could influence how researchers conceptualize learning in neural networks, potentially leading to new training methodologies and architectural designs.
    * The identification of environmental predictability as a key factor shaping ICL/IWL balance could guide the development of more robust and adaptive models.
    * The insights into learning dynamics and transience could inform strategies for continual learning and lifelong learning in Transformers.
    * While it could inform training of ICL in novel tasks, the limitation of the task domain would limit its immediate application.

**Overall Score:** 6/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel analogy and a well-executed experimental framework, leading to valuable insights into the ICL/IWL relationship. However, its reliance on simplified tasks, relatively high-level explanation, and reliance on analogy limit its immediate impact on real-world applications. The paper tackles an important question, is clearly presented, and provides a new perspective, but the general novelty and impact are limited by these factors.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Mission Balance: Generating Under-represented Class Samples using Video Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09858v1)
- **Authors**: Danush Kumar Venkatesh, Isabel Funke, Micha Pfeiffer, Fiona Kolbinger, Hanna Maria Schmeiser, Juergen Weitz, Marius Distler, Stefanie Speidel
- **Abstract**: Computer-assisted interventions can improve intra-operative guidance, particularly through deep learning methods that harness the spatiotemporal information in surgical videos. However, the severe data imbalance often found in surgical video datasets hinders the development of high-performing models. In this work, we aim to overcome the data imbalance by synthesizing surgical videos. We propose a unique two-stage, text-conditioned diffusion-based method to generate high-fidelity surgical videos for under-represented classes. Our approach conditions the generation process on text prompts and decouples spatial and temporal modeling by utilizing a 2D latent diffusion model to capture spatial content and then integrating temporal attention layers to ensure temporal consistency. Furthermore, we introduce a rejection sampling strategy to select the most suitable synthetic samples, effectively augmenting existing datasets to address class imbalance. We evaluate our method on two downstream tasks-surgical action recognition and intra-operative event prediction-demonstrating that incorporating synthetic videos from our approach substantially enhances model performance. We open-source our implementation at https://gitlab.com/nct_tso_public/surgvgen.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Mission Balance: Generating Under-represented Class Samples using Video Diffusion Models" addresses the data imbalance problem in surgical video datasets, which hinders the performance of deep learning models for computer-assisted interventions (CAI).  The authors propose SurV-Gen, a two-stage, text-conditioned video diffusion model to generate high-fidelity synthetic surgical videos for under-represented classes.  The first stage fine-tunes a pre-trained Stable Diffusion (SD) model on video frames. The second stage adds temporal transformer layers to the SD model to capture video dynamics, conditioned on text prompts and class labels.  A rejection sampling strategy is introduced to select the most suitable synthetic samples.  The authors evaluate their approach on surgical action recognition and intra-operative event prediction tasks, demonstrating improved model performance when synthetic videos are included in the training data. They are open-sourcing their implementation to facilitate further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper has reasonable novelty. The core idea of using video diffusion models to address data imbalance is not entirely new, but the specific implementation, including the two-stage approach and the use of rejection sampling, provides a novel contribution. The combination of a pre-trained image diffusion model (SD) with added temporal layers is a practical approach that leverages existing resources and improves training efficiency. Conditioning on both text prompts and class labels is also a valuable feature, enabling targeted generation of samples for specific under-represented classes.  The rejection sampling is a simple yet effective way to filter out potentially harmful synthetic data, addressing a known problem with generative models. The modular design, separating spatial and temporal characteristics, can be considered an important contribution to the efficiency and stability of generating high-resolution surgical video for augmentation.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the performance of deep learning models for surgical applications, which are often limited by data scarcity and imbalance. The ability to generate realistic surgical videos for under-represented classes has significant practical implications for surgical training, planning, and intra-operative guidance. By tackling the data imbalance problem directly, this work can help to develop more robust and reliable CAI systems.
The experiments clearly indicate that by combining SurV-Gen with rejection sampling the accuracy can be improved on downstream tasks.
The open-sourcing of the implementation is very important, since reproducibility can be tested and also the methodology can be extended to other CAI applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and motivates the data imbalance problem in surgical video analysis.
*   **Novel Approach:**  The proposed SurV-Gen method is a novel and practical solution that combines existing techniques in a useful way.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors evaluate their approach on two relevant downstream tasks, demonstrating the benefits of using synthetic data.
*   **Rejection Sampling:** The inclusion of a rejection sampling strategy is a significant strength, improving the quality and usefulness of the generated data.
*   **Open-Source Implementation:**  The open-sourcing of the code promotes reproducibility and further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Downstream Tasks:** The evaluation could be strengthened by considering a wider range of surgical tasks and datasets.
*   **Complexity of Text Prompts:** The paper does not fully elaborate on the text prompts used for conditioning, raising questions about how these prompts were designed and whether their quality affects the generated videos. How was the data used to generate meaningful text prompts? The generation relies on the assumption that the data contains enough meaningful information to be able to generate valuable text prompts to finetune the model.
*   **Image Quality Metrics:** While the paper included image quality metrics, there are no video-quality metrics, such as temporal smoothness or consistency. Temporal consistency is important in video generation and needs to be taken into consideration.
*   **Generalizability:** Does the approach work well with other surgical disciplines? Would the results be similar with other surgical video datasets?

**Score:**

Based on the above analysis, the paper warrants a score of **7/10**. The paper addresses a significant problem in the field with a reasonably novel approach, demonstrates the effectiveness of the method on relevant tasks, and provides the means for reproducibility. However, there is room for improvement in terms of the scope of the evaluation, further analysis of the text prompts, and inclusion of temporal consistency metrics.

### Unsupervised Radar Point Cloud Enhancement via Arbitrary LiDAR Guided Diffusion Prior
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09887v1)
- **Authors**: Yanlong Yang, Jianan Liu, Guanxiong Luo, Hao Li, Euijoon Ahn, Mostafa Rahimi Azghadi, Tao Huang
- **Abstract**: In industrial automation, radar is a critical sensor in machine perception. However, the angular resolution of radar is inherently limited by the Rayleigh criterion, which depends on both the radar's operating wavelength and the effective aperture of its antenna array.To overcome these hardware-imposed limitations, recent neural network-based methods have leveraged high-resolution LiDAR data, paired with radar measurements, during training to enhance radar point cloud resolution. While effective, these approaches require extensive paired datasets, which are costly to acquire and prone to calibration error. These challenges motivate the need for methods that can improve radar resolution without relying on paired high-resolution ground-truth data. Here, we introduce an unsupervised radar points enhancement algorithm that employs an arbitrary LiDAR-guided diffusion model as a prior without the need for paired training data. Specifically, our approach formulates radar angle estimation recovery as an inverse problem and incorporates prior knowledge through a diffusion model with arbitrary LiDAR domain knowledge. Experimental results demonstrate that our method attains high fidelity and low noise performance compared to traditional regularization techniques. Additionally, compared to paired training methods, it not only achieves comparable performance but also offers improved generalization capability. To our knowledge, this is the first approach that enhances radar points output by integrating prior knowledge via a diffusion model rather than relying on paired training data. Our code is available at https://github.com/yyxr75/RadarINV.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an unsupervised method for enhancing radar point cloud resolution using a diffusion model guided by arbitrary LiDAR data.  Unlike supervised approaches, this method doesn't require paired radar-LiDAR training data. The approach formulates radar angle estimation as an inverse problem and incorporates prior knowledge through a diffusion model trained on LiDAR data.  The method models the radar imaging process and solves it using a Bayesian framework, leveraging the diffusion model as a prior distribution. Experimental results on RADIal and K-Radar datasets demonstrate competitive performance compared to supervised methods and improved generalization capabilities. The code is available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the unsupervised approach using a diffusion model *guided by arbitrary LiDAR data* as a prior for radar point cloud enhancement.  Previous diffusion model-based radar enhancement methods typically relied on paired radar-LiDAR data for training the diffusion model. Bypassing the need for paired data is a significant advantage, addressing a practical limitation of existing techniques. Integrating a diffusion prior *in the inverse problem setting* for radar data enhancement is also a novel contribution. However, the underlying diffusion model architecture itself isn't groundbreaking, as it builds upon existing latent diffusion models.  The paper also claims to be the first successful application of such an approach in the context of radar point enhancement, which adds to its novelty.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the challenges of data acquisition and calibration in radar-LiDAR fusion.  By eliminating the need for paired data, the proposed method reduces costs and increases robustness to sensor misalignment or LiDAR data dropout. This makes the method more practical for real-world applications, especially in cost-sensitive scenarios or environments where LiDAR data quality is unreliable. The demonstrated generalization capability on K-Radar dataset without retraining further enhances its practical significance.  Improved radar perception is crucial for autonomous driving and robotics, directly contributing to progress in these fields.  However, the high inference time could limit its immediate adoption in real-time applications, as noted by the authors. The ablation studies show that early stopping degrades the performance significantly. Further exploration on this trade-off is needed.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Unsupervised approach:** Addresses the practical limitations of paired radar-LiDAR training.
    * **Competitive performance:** Achieves results comparable to supervised methods on RADIal.
    * **Strong generalization:** Demonstrated on K-Radar with a fixed diffusion prior.
    * **Clear formulation:** Bayesian framework provides a solid theoretical foundation.
    * **Code availability:** Promotes reproducibility and further research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **High inference time:** Limits real-time applicability. While addressed in the appendix, this is still a major hurdle that is not sufficiently resolved.
    * **Dependence on LiDAR data:** While not *paired*, the method still requires a diffusion model trained on *some* LiDAR data. The performance of the enhancement is limited by the quality and characteristics of the LiDAR data used for training the prior.
    * **Qualitative Results:** While quantitative results are shown, certain aspects of the qualitative comparison could be improved.  A more detailed analysis of the failure cases and limitations is warranted.
    * **Incomplete comparisons.** While the paper compares the method with other supervised and unsupervised approaches, it would be good to compare it against other unsupervised methods based on domain adaptation as well.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and cost-effective radar perception systems. The unsupervised approach opens up new avenues for leveraging readily available LiDAR data to improve radar resolution without the expense and complexity of paired data collection. The method could be adapted and extended for other sensor fusion tasks.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with both theoretical and practical significance. The unsupervised approach using a diffusion prior represents a clear advance over existing methods. The competitive performance and strong generalization capabilities are well-supported by experimental results. However, the high inference time and reliance on some LiDAR data prevent a higher score. The paper provides a good foundation for future work.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Diffusion-SAFE: Shared Autonomy Framework with Diffusion for Safe Human-to-Robot Driving Handover
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09889v1)
- **Authors**: Yunxin Fan, Monroe Kennedy III
- **Abstract**: Safe handover in shared autonomy for vehicle control is well-established in modern vehicles. However, avoiding accidents often requires action several seconds in advance. This necessitates understanding human driver behavior and an expert control strategy for seamless intervention when a collision or unsafe state is predicted. We propose Diffusion-SAFE, a closed-loop shared autonomy framework leveraging diffusion models to: (1) predict human driving behavior for detection of potential risks, (2) generate safe expert trajectories, and (3) enable smooth handovers by blending human and expert policies over a short time horizon. Unlike prior works which use engineered score functions to rate driving performance, our approach enables both performance evaluation and optimal action sequence generation from demonstrations. By adjusting the forward and reverse processes of the diffusion-based copilot, our method ensures a gradual transition of control authority, by mimicking the drivers' behavior before intervention, which mitigates abrupt takeovers, leading to smooth transitions. We evaluated Diffusion-SAFE in both simulation (CarRacing-v0) and real-world (ROS-based race car), measuring human-driving similarity, safety, and computational efficiency. Results demonstrate a 98.5\% successful handover rate, highlighting the framework's effectiveness in progressively correcting human actions and continuously sampling optimal robot actions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents Diffusion-SAFE, a shared autonomy framework designed for safe human-to-robot driving handover. It uses diffusion models for two primary purposes: (1) an evaluator predicts the driver's intent and assesses potential risks, and (2) a copilot generates safe expert trajectories. The key innovation is the dynamic adjustment of a forward diffusion ratio to seamlessly blend human control with the autonomous copilot, mitigating abrupt takeovers. The framework is evaluated in both simulation and real-world ROS-based race car environments, demonstrating improvements in safety, human-driving similarity, and computational efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's main novelty lies in its specific application of diffusion models to the shared autonomy problem in driving, particularly with the dynamic adjustment of the forward diffusion ratio for smooth handover. While diffusion models have been used in robotics before, the tailored design for this specific application, combining intent prediction with safe trajectory generation and a gradual takeover mechanism, is a key distinguishing factor. The idea of adjusting a diffusion model's forward process to gradually transfer control authority is innovative. The use of separate expert and average demonstration datasets for copilot and evaluator respectively is a subtle, but thoughtful design choice.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in shared autonomy – the smooth and safe transition of control between a human operator and an autonomous system. The use of diffusion models to address the inherent uncertainty and multimodality of human behavior is compelling.  By enabling gradual takeovers, the system could improve user acceptance and reduce the risk of accidents. The empirical validation in both simulation and real-world settings strengthens the claim of practical applicability.  If the framework holds up to more complex real-world driving scenarios, it could contribute significantly to the development of more reliable and user-friendly shared autonomy systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of shared autonomy in driving and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The methodology is well-explained and based on established principles of diffusion modeling and control.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The use of both simulation and real-world experiments provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the framework.  The results are quantified with appropriate metrics.
    *   **Well-Motivated Design Choices:** The paper justifies the design choices, such as using different datasets for training the evaluator and copilot, and adjusting the forward diffusion ratio.
    *   **Comprehensive Ablation Study:** The ablation study thoroughly analyzes the impact of different model components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Real-World Complexity:** The real-world experiments are conducted in a controlled race car environment, which may not fully represent the complexity of real-world driving scenarios. While the ROS setup does give it added credibility, and its still commendable, the real-world environment needs to be considered.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models can be computationally expensive, particularly during inference. The paper mentions computation time as a performance metric, but doesn't fully explore the implications for real-time applications, where response time is critical. More analysis on the computational scalability of the approach would improve the paper.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to SOTA Shared Autonomy Frameworks:** While the paper compares to baselines specific to components of the system (e.g. LSTMs for behavior modeling), a direct comparison against an end-to-end shared autonomy framework would highlight the relative performance advantages of Diffusion-SAFE.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more sophisticated and user-friendly shared autonomy systems. The use of diffusion models for intent prediction and safe trajectory generation could be adopted in other domains beyond driving. The gradual takeover mechanism is also a valuable contribution that could improve user acceptance of autonomous systems.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7.5/10**.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to shared autonomy in driving using diffusion models. The concept of dynamically adjusting the forward diffusion ratio for smooth handover is a significant contribution. The empirical validation in both simulation and real-world settings strengthens the claim of practical applicability. However, the limitations related to real-world complexity, computational cost, and lack of direct comparison to SOTA shared autonomy frameworks prevent it from scoring higher. Further research is needed to address these limitations and fully demonstrate the potential of Diffusion-SAFE in more realistic and challenging driving scenarios. The potential of the approach, however, is clearly demonstrated, warranting the solid 7.5 score.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 0/10

### Comparing Exploration-Exploitation Strategies of LLMs and Humans: Insights from Standard Multi-armed Bandit Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09901v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyuan Zhang, Darcy Wang, Ningyuan Chen, Rodrigo Mansur, Vahid Sarhangian
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to simulate or automate human behavior in complex sequential decision-making tasks. A natural question is then whether LLMs exhibit similar decision-making behavior to humans, and can achieve comparable (or superior) performance. In this work, we focus on the exploration-exploitation (E&E) tradeoff, a fundamental aspect of dynamic decision-making under uncertainty. We employ canonical multi-armed bandit (MAB) tasks introduced in the cognitive science and psychiatry literature to conduct a comparative study of the E&E strategies of LLMs, humans, and MAB algorithms. We use interpretable choice models to capture the E&E strategies of the agents and investigate how explicit reasoning, through both prompting strategies and reasoning-enhanced models, shapes LLM decision-making. We find that reasoning shifts LLMs toward more human-like behavior, characterized by a mix of random and directed exploration. In simple stationary tasks, reasoning-enabled LLMs exhibit similar levels of random and directed exploration compared to humans. However, in more complex, non-stationary environments, LLMs struggle to match human adaptability, particularly in effective directed exploration, despite achieving similar regret in certain scenarios. Our findings highlight both the promise and limits of LLMs as simulators of human behavior and tools for automated decision-making and point to potential areas of improvements.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how large language models (LLMs) approach the exploration-exploitation (E&E) tradeoff in multi-armed bandit (MAB) tasks, comparing their strategies to those of humans and traditional MAB algorithms. Using two standard MAB tasks (stationary 2-armed and non-stationary 4-armed), the authors analyze the decision-making behavior of several commercial LLMs, human participants, and algorithmic baselines. They model agents' choices using interpretable choice models and examine the impact of reasoning (via prompt engineering and reasoning-enhanced architectures) on LLM behavior. The study reveals that while basic LLMs exhibit more random exploration and less directed exploration than humans, reasoning capabilities can shift LLMs towards more human-like strategies, particularly in stationary tasks. However, LLMs still struggle to match human adaptability in complex, non-stationary environments. The paper highlights both the potential and limitations of LLMs as simulators of human behavior and tools for automated decision-making, identifying areas for improvement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Comparative Study:** The paper provides a valuable comparative analysis of LLM, human, and algorithm E&E strategies across different task complexities.  This is a significant contribution, as previous studies often focused on simpler setups.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of established MAB tasks from cognitive science, standard interpretable choice models, and a hierarchical Bayesian approach for parameter estimation strengthens the validity and reliability of the findings.  Using human behavioral data alongside LLM outputs is a major strength.
*   **Focus on Reasoning:** The examination of reasoning's impact on LLM decision-making is a crucial aspect of the study. By explicitly incorporating reasoning capabilities, the authors uncover how it influences exploration strategies and brings LLM behavior closer to that of humans.
*   **Clear Interpretation of Results:** The paper offers a clear and insightful interpretation of the observed differences in E&E strategies, highlighting both the promise and limits of LLMs. The discussion of the implications for LLM use in dynamic decision-making tasks is useful.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Human Dataset Demographics:** The human behavioral data does not include demographic information. This limits the extent to which the LLM data can be compared to the general population.
*   **Reliance on Prompting:** While prompting is an important tool, it is highly sensitive to formulation and may not fully reveal LLMs' inherent capabilities. The conclusions about LLM behavior may be somewhat tied to the specific prompts used.
*   **Model limitations:** This is a good approach to modeling the decision making process but it is worth noting that it makes assumptions about what influences the decisions and ignores a lot of other sources of potential influence. The results therefore only represent those sources that the model includes.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research on LLM behavior and decision-making. While some previous studies have examined E&E tradeoffs in LLMs, this work is more comprehensive in its comparative approach, focusing on the types of human behavioral data used as well as the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.  The focus on reasoning is a timely and relevant contribution, given the recent advances in this area.  The finding that explicit reasoning can improve LLM E&E strategies, but that challenges remain in complex environments, offers a nuanced perspective.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. This score is justified by the paper's strengths in providing a comprehensive comparative study and rigorously investigating the role of reasoning in LLM decision-making. The findings contribute to the understanding of LLMs as potential simulators of human behavior and tools for automated decision-making. However, the limitations related to human data and the potential bias introduced by prompting prevent a higher score.
The paper is solid, well-executed, and makes a contribution to the field but the limitations outlined prevent it from being an exceptional and transformative piece of work.
Score: 7
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Crossing Borders Without Crossing Boundaries: How Sociolinguistic Awareness Can Optimize User Engagement with Localized Spanish AI Models Across Hispanophone Countries
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09902v1)
- **Authors**: Martin Capdevila, Esteban Villa Turek, Ellen Karina Chumbe Fernandez, Luis Felipe Polo Galvez, Luis Cadavid, Andrea Marroquin, Rebeca Vargas Quesada, Johanna Crew, Nicole Vallejo Galarraga, Christopher Rodriguez, Diego Gutierrez, Radhi Datla
- **Abstract**: Large language models are, by definition, based on language. In an effort to underscore the critical need for regional localized models, this paper examines primary differences between variants of written Spanish across Latin America and Spain, with an in-depth sociocultural and linguistic contextualization therein. We argue that these differences effectively constitute significant gaps in the quotidian use of Spanish among dialectal groups by creating sociolinguistic dissonances, to the extent that locale-sensitive AI models would play a pivotal role in bridging these divides. In doing so, this approach informs better and more efficient localization strategies that also serve to more adequately meet inclusivity goals, while securing sustainable active daily user growth in a major low-risk investment geographic area. Therefore, implementing at least the proposed five sub variants of Spanish addresses two lines of action: to foment user trust and reliance on AI language models while also demonstrating a level of cultural, historical, and sociolinguistic awareness that reflects positively on any internationalization strategy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper argues for the necessity of localized Spanish language models, rather than a single, homogenized one, to optimize user engagement and inclusivity across Hispanophone countries. It highlights significant differences in written Spanish across Latin America and Spain due to diverse influences (Indigenous languages, colloquialisms, historical factors), leading to sociolinguistic dissonance. The authors propose implementing at least five sub-variants of Spanish models to foster user trust, demonstrate cultural awareness, and improve internationalization strategies. The paper discusses various linguistic differences (grammatical, lexical, slang, morphology) and sociocultural influences (Indigenous languages, pop culture), advocating for a nuanced approach to language model development that acknowledges regional variations. They propose five core groupings of Spanish variants based on regions (Miami Spanish, Spain Spanish, Mexico Spanish, and others) as a base for localized models. The authors contend that a localized approach is essential for increased user reach, engagement, and retention, and avoiding a "colonizing" or erasure effect imposed by a single standardized model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's core argument – that localized Spanish language models are crucial for user engagement – is *not entirely novel*. The general principle that language models perform better when tailored to specific dialects or locales is well-established in NLP.  However, the paper presents a fairly comprehensive compilation of linguistic and sociocultural factors relevant to Spanish, providing a specific justification for *why* localization is so important in this particular language context. This specificity, and particularly the attention to cultural nuances and indigenous influences is one of its strengths.

**Significance:**  The paper's significance lies in its practical implications for developers of Spanish language AI. By providing specific examples of linguistic differences and proposing initial region groupings, it offers a starting point for building more effective and inclusive models. The focus on *written* language variants is relevant, given that many current approaches focus primarily on spoken language differentiation. The arguments for avoiding a "colonizing" approach and considering sociocultural factors are also valuable, aligning with current trends in responsible AI development. The emphasis on potential business benefits (increased user adoption) could convince developers to adopt a more nuanced localized approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Empirical Evidence:** The paper primarily offers a theoretical justification for localization. It lacks robust empirical data demonstrating the performance improvement of localized models compared to a standardized one. Quantitative comparisons would significantly strengthen the argument.
*   **Overly Broad Groupings:** While proposing five core groupings is a good start, it is a simplified approach. Within each grouping, considerable regional variation exists. A more granular model might be necessary for optimal performance.
*   **Miami-centric View:** The paper's strong focus on Miami Spanish as a representative variant may be somewhat biased. While Miami is linguistically diverse, it doesn't necessarily reflect the most representative form of Spanish for *all* contexts or user groups. This might inadvertently create a new kind of bias.
*   **Limited Technical Depth:** While outlining *why* localized models are needed, the paper offers relatively little detail on *how* to technically implement these models. Practical guidance on data collection, training strategies, and evaluation metrics for localized Spanish would be valuable.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a reasonable argument for the necessity of localized Spanish language models, highlighting the nuanced linguistic and sociocultural landscape of Hispanophone countries and the need for avoiding linguistic erasure and "colonializing" AI practices. This has some novelty, but largely follows on from established literature that covers language variations in other regions, languages, and cultures. The argument for better ROI and marketing by following the method also is valuable. The paper has a lot of theoretical/conceptual work, but lacks a lot of empirical/technical work, which would need to be the next step for any business or researcher who wanted to follow through on the recommendation.

Overall, the paper contributes to the discussion around inclusivity and localization in AI, but is more informative and suggestive of needed approaches, and less a fully fleshed out scientific methodology.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### UICopilot: Automating UI Synthesis via Hierarchical Code Generation from Webpage Designs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09904v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Gui, Yao Wan, Zhen Li, Zhongyi Zhang, Dongping Chen, Hongyu Zhang, Yi Su, Bohua Chen, Xing Zhou, Wenbin Jiang, Xiangliang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Automating the synthesis of User Interfaces (UIs) plays a crucial role in enhancing productivity and accelerating the development lifecycle, reducing both development time and manual effort. Recently, the rapid development of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has made it possible to generate front-end Hypertext Markup Language (HTML) code directly from webpage designs. However, real-world webpages encompass not only a diverse array of HTML tags but also complex stylesheets, resulting in significantly lengthy code. The lengthy code poses challenges for the performance and efficiency of MLLMs, especially in capturing the structural information of UI designs. To address these challenges, this paper proposes UICopilot, a novel approach to automating UI synthesis via hierarchical code generation from webpage designs. The core idea of UICopilot is to decompose the generation process into two stages: first, generating the coarse-grained HTML hierarchical structure, followed by the generation of fine-grained code. To validate the effectiveness of UICopilot, we conduct experiments on a real-world dataset, i.e., WebCode2M. Experimental results demonstrate that UICopilot significantly outperforms existing baselines in both automatic evaluation metrics and human evaluations. Specifically, statistical analysis reveals that the majority of human annotators prefer the webpages generated by UICopilot over those produced by GPT-4V.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UICOPILOT, a novel approach to automatically generating UI code from webpage designs (screenshots). It addresses the challenges of lengthy code generation and complex nested structures often found in real-world webpages, which can hinder the performance of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). UICOPILOT decomposes the code generation process into two stages: 1) generating a coarse-grained HTML hierarchical structure (DOM tree) with bounding boxes (BBoxes), and 2) generating fine-grained HTML/CSS code for individual regions and overall styling. A Vision Transformer (ViT)-based structure model predicts the coarse DOM tree, and GPT-4V is used as a code generation agent for the fine-grained details.  The approach utilizes a BBox-based pruning strategy to simplify the dataset and reduce complexity. Experiments on the WebCode2M dataset demonstrate that UICOPILOT outperforms existing baselines in automatic evaluation metrics and human evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its two-stage hierarchical approach to UI code generation. While the individual components (ViT, GPT-4V) are not new, the way they're combined to address the specific challenges of webpage generation appears to be a significant contribution. Breaking down the problem into coarse structure prediction and fine-grained detail generation is a logical and effective way to manage the complexity of real-world webpage designs. This decoupling enables more accurate and efficient handling of lengthy code and deeply nested structures. The introduction of BBox-based data pruning also adds a practical improvement to the pipeline. The use of this pre-processing step is unique to this task and enhances the effectiveness of the subsequent MLLM.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to streamline front-end development and reduce manual effort.  Automated UI synthesis is a valuable goal, and UICOPILOT appears to make substantial progress in that direction. The experimental results are compelling, showing improvements over existing baselines (including direct GPT-4V).  The human evaluation further strengthens the claim that UICOPILOT generates more visually accurate webpages. The architecture and results have demonstrated that the decoupling method significantly improves code generation quality in complex webpage scenarios, making this technology applicable to a variety of use cases.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a clear problem:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of existing one-step approaches for UI code generation.
    *   **Well-defined approach:** The hierarchical architecture and the role of each component are well-defined.
    *   **Compelling experimental results:**  The quantitative results (CLIP, SSIM, Visual Score) demonstrate significant improvements. The human evaluation adds further validation.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The ablation studies (UICOPILOT w/o opt) and the parameter search for *min_area* and *max_depth* provide valuable insights into the performance of the different components.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on GPT-4V:**  While using GPT-4V is practical, it also limits the reproducibility and accessibility of the work for researchers who don't have access to the API. An exploration of open-source models would broaden the impact. The cost of calling GPT-4V in this two-step architecture should have been included.
    *   **Scope Limitation:** The paper focuses on static webpages and excludes JavaScript. While this is a reasonable starting point, the real-world applicability would be further enhanced by addressing dynamic elements.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** WebCode2M, while real-world, has inherent biases and limitations (e.g., the cleansing process mentioned).  Acknowledging these biases more explicitly would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited code example analysis:** While the paper mentions some limitations observed after the post-generation editing, it could have benefited from more examples on failure cases and areas where more improvement could be reached.

*   **Impact:**  UICOPILOT has the potential to significantly impact the field of UI development automation. By overcoming the limitations of one-step code generation, it opens up avenues for more efficient and accurate code synthesis from design inputs. The work can inspire future research in hierarchical approaches and specialized MLLM architectures for UI generation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a challenging problem. The results are compelling, and the analysis provides valuable insights. However, the reliance on GPT-4V, the limited scope (excluding Javascript), and the acknowledged dataset biases temper the impact slightly. While the two-stage approach is novel, the building blocks are not.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### PIG: Privacy Jailbreak Attack on LLMs via Gradient-based Iterative In-Context Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09921v1)
- **Authors**: Yidan Wang, Yanan Cao, Yubing Ren, Fang Fang, Zheng Lin, Binxing Fang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel in various domains but pose inherent privacy risks. Existing methods to evaluate privacy leakage in LLMs often use memorized prefixes or simple instructions to extract data, both of which well-alignment models can easily block. Meanwhile, Jailbreak attacks bypass LLM safety mechanisms to generate harmful content, but their role in privacy scenarios remains underexplored. In this paper, we examine the effectiveness of jailbreak attacks in extracting sensitive information, bridging privacy leakage and jailbreak attacks in LLMs. Moreover, we propose PIG, a novel framework targeting Personally Identifiable Information (PII) and addressing the limitations of current jailbreak methods. Specifically, PIG identifies PII entities and their types in privacy queries, uses in-context learning to build a privacy context, and iteratively updates it with three gradient-based strategies to elicit target PII. We evaluate PIG and existing jailbreak methods using two privacy-related datasets. Experiments on four white-box and two black-box LLMs show that PIG outperforms baseline methods and achieves state-of-the-art (SoTA) results. The results underscore significant privacy risks in LLMs, emphasizing the need for stronger safeguards. Our code is availble at \href{https://github.com/redwyd/PrivacyJailbreak}{https://github.com/redwyd/PrivacyJailbreak}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PIG: Privacy Jailbreak Attack on LLMs via Gradient-based Iterative In-Context Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PIG, a novel framework designed to elicit Personally Identifiable Information (PII) from Large Language Models (LLMs) using jailbreak techniques. PIG leverages the in-context learning capabilities of LLMs, combined with gradient-based iterative optimization, to craft effective privacy jailbreak attacks. The framework identifies PII entities and their types within privacy-related queries, uses this information to build a privacy context, and then iteratively refines this context using three different gradient-based strategies to maximize the likelihood of extracting sensitive information. The paper evaluates PIG against existing jailbreak methods on two privacy-related datasets and demonstrates its superior performance on both white-box and black-box LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach by explicitly bridging jailbreak attacks and privacy leakage. Most prior work in jailbreaking focuses on generating harmful content, while privacy implications have been largely overlooked. PIG addresses this gap by tailoring jailbreak techniques to extract sensitive information.
*   **Technical Contribution:** The core contribution is the PIG framework itself, specifically the use of gradient-based iterative optimization within an in-context learning paradigm. This iterative refinement of the privacy context is a key innovation that improves attack success rates. The three gradient-based strategies (random, entity, and dynamic) provide flexibility and adaptability.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation on multiple LLMs (both white-box and black-box) and datasets. The comparison with various baselines, including established jailbreak methods, provides strong evidence of PIG's effectiveness. The ablation study analyzing the contributions of in-context learning and token selection strategies further strengthens the analysis.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The demonstrated privacy risks in LLMs highlight the need for stronger safeguards and increase awareness among researchers and developers.
* **Detailed Analysis**: The inclusion of case studies (Section F) and the further analysis of 29 Jailbreak methods from Wei et al. (2024a) provides further depth.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Reference Responses:** The gradient-based optimization relies on crafting reference responses. While the paper acknowledges this, the choice of reference response could influence the optimization process and potentially limit generalizability. There could be more analysis regarding the sensitivity of the optimization process to the specific reference response used.
*   **Computational Cost:** Gradient-based optimization, particularly with multiple strategies, can be computationally expensive. The paper acknowledges this but doesn't delve into detailed efficiency analysis or potential optimizations. While it's demonstrated that PIG converges quicker than GCG, more detail on the computational requirements would add strength.
*   **Domain Specificity:** The datasets used are specific to privacy-related queries. While this is the focus of the paper, the performance of PIG in other domains, or its adaptability to different types of sensitive information, is not explored. Expanding the evaluation to more diverse scenarios would strengthen the findings.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** Although the authors state that all data is randomly generated to avoid exposing real individual's privacy, the paper acknowledges the dual-use nature of this research. However, it would be appropriate to explicitly delve deeper into discussing potential misuses and limitations for preventing malicious usage of PIG, beyond just releasing code and results. This is particularly relevant considering LLMs are commonly used by the general public who may not have the expertise to protect themselves.

**Significance:**

The paper's findings have significant implications for the security and privacy of LLMs. It demonstrates that even well-aligned models are vulnerable to sophisticated jailbreak attacks tailored for extracting sensitive information. The PIG framework provides a powerful tool for assessing and mitigating these privacy risks. The work contributes to a growing body of research focused on the vulnerabilities of LLMs and emphasizes the importance of developing robust privacy safeguards. The combination of in-context learning with gradient based methods represents a valuable contribution to the area.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by bridging the gap between jailbreak attacks and privacy leakage in LLMs. The novelty of the PIG framework, its strong experimental evaluation, and its practical relevance justify a good score. However, the reliance on reference responses, the computational cost, and domain specificity of the evaluation limit the paper's overall impact. More rigorous ethical considerations should be present. Addressing these limitations in future work could significantly enhance the significance and generalizability of this research.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving the Euclidean Diffusion Generation of Manifold Data by Mitigating Score Function Singularity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09922v1)
- **Authors**: Zichen Liu, Wei Zhang, Tiejun Li
- **Abstract**: Euclidean diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in generative modeling across diverse domains, and they have been extended to manifold case in recent advances. Instead of explicitly utilizing the structure of special manifolds as studied in previous works, we investigate direct sampling of the Euclidean diffusion models for general manifold-constrained data in this paper. We reveal the multiscale singularity of the score function in the embedded space of manifold, which hinders the accuracy of diffusion-generated samples. We then present an elaborate theoretical analysis of the singularity structure of the score function by separating it along the tangential and normal directions of the manifold. To mitigate the singularity and improve the sampling accuracy, we propose two novel methods: (1) Niso-DM, which introduces non-isotropic noise along the normal direction to reduce scale discrepancies, and (2) Tango-DM, which trains only the tangential component of the score function using a tangential-only loss function. Numerical experiments demonstrate that our methods achieve superior performance on distributions over various manifolds with complex geometries.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of generating data on manifolds using Euclidean diffusion models.  It identifies a key challenge: the singularity of the score function in the ambient Euclidean space, which hinders the accurate approximation of the score function using neural networks. This singularity arises due to the disparity in scales between the tangential and normal components of the score function relative to the manifold. To mitigate this, the authors propose two methods: (1) Niso-DM, which introduces non-isotropic noise perturbation in the normal direction to reduce scale discrepancy, and (2) Tango-DM, which trains only the tangential component of the score function using a specially designed loss function.  The paper provides theoretical analysis of the multiscale structure of the score function, and experimental results demonstrate the superior performance of the proposed methods compared to vanilla Euclidean diffusion models on several benchmark manifolds.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its detailed theoretical analysis of the score function singularity for manifolds embedded in Euclidean space, specifically separating the tangential and normal components. Previous work identified the singularity, but this paper provides a more granular breakdown.  The proposed Niso-DM and Tango-DM are also novel methods for addressing this singularity. Tango-DM seems particularly interesting as it fundamentally changes the training objective.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of generating data on manifolds is significant in many scientific fields. Directly applying Euclidean diffusion models is an intuitive, yet often underperforming approach. Improving their performance, as this paper demonstrates, is a valuable contribution. The analysis and proposed methods are likely to be relevant to other researchers working on diffusion models and generative modeling on manifolds. The work also provides insights that could be applicable to other manifold learning techniques.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Solid Theoretical Analysis:** The paper presents a rigorous theoretical analysis of the score function's singularity.
    *   **Well-Motivated Methods:** The proposed Niso-DM and Tango-DM are well-motivated by the theoretical analysis and address a key bottleneck in the application of Euclidean diffusion models to manifold data.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments cover a range of manifolds, including both toy models and more complex systems.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, the proposed methods, and the experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Comparison to Riemannian Diffusion Models:** While the paper justifies using Euclidean diffusion models as an alternative to costly Riemannian methods, a more direct comparison to state-of-the-art Riemannian diffusion models would strengthen the paper.  Even a discussion comparing and contrasting the results against Riemannian models would be beneficial.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper acknowledges sensitivity to hyperparameters. A more in-depth study on hyperparameter selection and a guide to choosing appropriate values for different manifolds would be valuable.
    *   **Computational Complexity:** The paper could benefit from a discussion of the computational complexity of the proposed methods, especially Tango-DM, which involves projecting the score function. While avoiding geometric computations on the manifold is a motivation, a comparison of complexity would be helpful.
    *   **Practical Limitations**:  The analysis of Niso-DM in particular assumes *knowledge of the normal space* to the manifold, which may not be known in practice. The practical applicability of these methods should be better addressed.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of generative modeling on manifolds. While the theoretical analysis is strong and novel, and the proposed methods are well-motivated, the *practical* novelty seems limited by the need for knowing the normal space of the manifold and the added complexity of the models.  The lack of comparison against other manifold learning methods is also a shortcoming. The presented methods are novel but are not a "breakthrough" approach, and the experiments demonstrate improvement in several settings. Therefore, a score in the middle range is appropriate.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### From Trade-off to Synergy: A Versatile Symbiotic Watermarking Framework for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09924v1)
- **Authors**: Yidan Wang, Yubing Ren, Yanan Cao, Binxing Fang
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) has heightened concerns about the misuse of AI-generated text, making watermarking a promising solution. Mainstream watermarking schemes for LLMs fall into two categories: logits-based and sampling-based. However, current schemes entail trade-offs among robustness, text quality, and security. To mitigate this, we integrate logits-based and sampling-based schemes, harnessing their respective strengths to achieve synergy. In this paper, we propose a versatile symbiotic watermarking framework with three strategies: serial, parallel, and hybrid. The hybrid framework adaptively embeds watermarks using token entropy and semantic entropy, optimizing the balance between detectability, robustness, text quality, and security. Furthermore, we validate our approach through comprehensive experiments on various datasets and models. Experimental results indicate that our method outperforms existing baselines and achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. We believe this framework provides novel insights into diverse watermarking paradigms. Our code is available at \href{https://github.com/redwyd/SymMark}{https://github.com/redwyd/SymMark}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SymMark, a novel symbiotic watermarking framework for large language models (LLMs). It addresses the trade-offs between robustness, text quality, and security inherent in existing watermarking schemes (logits-based and sampling-based). SymMark integrates these two approaches through three strategies: Serial, Parallel, and Hybrid. The Hybrid approach adaptively embeds watermarks based on token and semantic entropy, optimizing the balance between detectability, robustness, quality, and security.  Extensive experiments demonstrate SymMark's superior performance compared to existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *symbiotic* integration of logits-based and sampling-based watermarking. While individual watermarking techniques are well-established, the idea of combining them in an adaptive, entropy-driven manner is a significant step forward. The three integration strategies (Serial, Parallel, and Hybrid) offer flexibility and allow for trade-off management. The concept of using entropy to guide watermark placement is also innovative.

*   **Significance:** Watermarking is becoming increasingly critical for responsible LLM deployment. The work directly addresses a key challenge: the existing trade-offs that limit the practicality of watermarking. If SymMark can robustly overcome these limitations, it could have a considerable impact. Improved watermarking strengthens the accountability of LLM outputs, potentially mitigating the spread of misinformation, plagiarism, and harmful content generation. The work aims for a more robust and secure method for identifying AI-generated text which makes this research timely, relevant, and important.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper offers a systematic exploration of different watermarking paradigms and provides a well-defined framework for integrating them.
    *   **Adaptive Mechanism:** Entropy-based adaptation allows the system to dynamically optimize watermark embedding, addressing a limitation of static methods.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough, covering various datasets, models, and adversarial attacks.  The comparison against numerous baselines strengthens the claims.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and the concepts are clearly explained. Figures and tables are used effectively to present the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The Hybrid approach, while effective, is more complex than existing watermarking methods.  The overhead of entropy calculation could be a concern in resource-constrained environments.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The entropy thresholds (alpha and beta) are important hyperparameters. While the authors explore these parameters to some degree, it is important to note potential sensitivities to the choice of hyperparameter.

*   **Impact:** The paper's potential influence depends on the real-world effectiveness of SymMark in complex adversarial environments and its adaptability across a wide range of LLMs and tasks. It has the potential to stimulate further research in adaptive watermarking and the symbiotic use of existing techniques. The open-source implementation of the code and method further allows future study and reproducibility.

**Justification for Score:**

This paper presents a sound advancement in the field of LLM watermarking. While it builds upon existing work, it provides a novel and well-executed integration strategy that tackles an important practical challenge, and offers a robust solution with strong empirical evidence. The weaknesses primarily revolve around complexity and potential overhead, rather than fundamental flaws. Given this, and the current urgency of the problem, I'd rate it a **Score: 7**. This score indicates a significant and impactful contribution, demonstrating novelty and showing strong potential for future impact, though some aspects of the work require additional validation and further simplification.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reinforced Interactive Continual Learning via Real-time Noisy Human Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09925v1)
- **Authors**: Yutao Yang, Jie Zhou, Junsong Li, Qianjun Pan, Bihao Zhan, Qin Chen, Xipeng Qiu, Liang He
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces an interactive continual learning paradigm where AI models dynamically learn new skills from real-time human feedback while retaining prior knowledge. This paradigm distinctively addresses two major limitations of traditional continual learning: (1) dynamic model updates using streaming, real-time human-annotated data, rather than static datasets with fixed labels, and (2) the assumption of clean labels, by explicitly handling the noisy feedback common in real-world interactions. To tackle these problems, we propose RiCL, a Reinforced interactive Continual Learning framework leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to learn new skills effectively from dynamic feedback. RiCL incorporates three key components: a temporal consistency-aware purifier to automatically discern clean from noisy samples in data streams; an interaction-aware direct preference optimization strategy to align model behavior with human intent by reconciling AI-generated and human-provided feedback; and a noise-resistant contrastive learning module that captures robust representations by exploiting inherent data relationships, thus avoiding reliance on potentially unreliable labels. Extensive experiments on two benchmark datasets (FewRel and TACRED), contaminated with realistic noise patterns, demonstrate that our RiCL approach substantially outperforms existing combinations of state-of-the-art online continual learning and noisy-label learning methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RiCL, a "Reinforced Interactive Continual Learning" framework, designed for AI models to learn continuously from real-time, noisy human feedback. RiCL tackles two key limitations of traditional continual learning: (1) the reliance on static datasets with fixed labels, and (2) the assumption of clean labels, which is unrealistic in real-world interactions. RiCL consists of three main components: a Temporal Consistency-Aware Purifier (TCP) to distinguish clean and noisy data, an Interaction-Aware Direct Preference Optimization (IPO) strategy to align model behavior with human intent, and a Noise-Resistant Contrastive Learning (NCL) module to learn robust representations, avoiding reliance on unreliable labels. The paper presents experimental results on FewRel and Tacred datasets, demonstrating that RiCL outperforms existing online continual learning and noisy label learning methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the integrated approach to address the specific challenges of interactive continual learning with noisy human feedback. While the individual components (TCP, IPO, NCL) may draw inspiration from existing techniques, their combination and adaptation for this specific paradigm is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Strengths:** The framework's modularity is a significant strength. It allows for potential enhancements or replacements of individual components without affecting the overall architecture. The use of reinforcement learning, specifically DPO, to align model behavior with human intent is also a clever idea and relatively novel in the continual learning context.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The experimental setup relies on *simulated* noisy human feedback.  While the authors try to introduce realistic noise patterns, it's still a far cry from actual human interactions, which could involve biases, inconsistencies, and nuanced communication. This weakens the practical relevance of the results. Furthermore, the datasets used (FewRel and Tacred) might not fully represent the complexity and diversity of real-world interactive scenarios.  The paper also lacks an in-depth analysis of the limitations and potential failure modes of the proposed method. How does RiCL perform when the human feedback is *intentionally* misleading or adversarial?  How sensitive is the framework to the choice of hyperparameters for each component?  A more thorough analysis of these aspects would significantly strengthen the work.
    *   **Integration of existing components:**  While combining techniques is a valid approach, it also raises questions about the incremental contribution of each component, relative to using other techniques of the same kind. The paper lacks a detailed comparison to alternative methods for each component's individual function, which would help justify their choice.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses an important and relevant problem: how to build AI systems that can learn effectively from continuous interaction with humans, even when the feedback is noisy. If the results generalize to more realistic scenarios, RiCL could have a significant impact on developing more robust and adaptive AI systems.
    *   **Strengths:**  The paper identifies and addresses a gap in the existing literature by focusing on the interactive continual learning setting with noisy feedback. This is a more realistic and practical scenario than many traditional continual learning benchmarks.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The reliance on simulated data significantly limits the practical significance of the work. The impact on the research field will hinge on whether the approach can be successfully applied and validated in real-world applications with actual human users. The paper would benefit from a clearer articulation of the potential impact and applications of RiCL in specific domains (e.g., personalized education, assistive technologies, etc.).

*   **Clarity and Presentation:**  The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework and experimental results. The figures are helpful in understanding the architecture of RiCL.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **6.5/10**.

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of continual learning by addressing the critical challenge of learning from noisy human feedback in interactive settings. The integrated approach of RiCL, combining a purifier, a preference optimization strategy, and contrastive learning, is a novel and promising direction. However, the reliance on *simulated* noisy feedback is a major limitation that significantly undermines the practical significance of the work. The paper would be significantly strengthened by validation in more realistic settings and a more in-depth analysis of the framework's limitations and robustness. The paper's novelty and significance are thus moderate, and further research is needed to fully realize the potential of the RiCL framework. I have therefore assigned a score that sits slightly above the average rating but reflects the significant issues that I have identified.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 0/10

### Rethinking Prompt Optimizers: From Prompt Merits to Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09930v1)
- **Authors**: Zixiao Zhu, Hanzhang Zhou, Zijian Feng, Tianjiao Li, Chua Jia Jim Deryl, Mak Lee Onn, Gee Wah Ng, Kezhi Mao
- **Abstract**: Prompt optimization (PO) offers a practical alternative to fine-tuning large language models (LLMs), enabling performance improvements without altering model weights. Existing methods typically rely on advanced, large-scale LLMs like GPT-4 to generate optimized prompts. However, due to limited downward compatibility, verbose, instruction-heavy prompts from advanced LLMs can overwhelm lightweight inference models and degrade response quality. In this work, we rethink prompt optimization through the lens of interpretable design. We first identify a set of model-agnostic prompt quality merits and empirically validate their effectiveness in enhancing prompt and response quality. We then introduce MePO, a merit-guided, lightweight, and locally deployable prompt optimizer trained on our preference dataset built from merit-aligned prompts generated by a lightweight LLM. Unlike prior work, MePO avoids online optimization reliance, reduces cost and privacy concerns, and, by learning clear, interpretable merits, generalizes effectively to both large-scale and lightweight inference models. Experiments demonstrate that MePO achieves better results across diverse tasks and model types, offering a scalable and robust solution for real-world deployment. Our model and dataset are available at: https://github.com/MidiyaZhu/MePO
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Rethinking Prompt Optimizers: From Prompt Merits to Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current prompt optimization (PO) methods, which often rely on advanced, large-scale LLMs (e.g., GPT-4) to generate optimized prompts. The authors argue that these prompts are not always downward compatible, meaning they may overwhelm lightweight inference models and degrade response quality. The paper proposes a novel approach that focuses on interpretable prompt design merits and uses lightweight LLMs for prompt optimization.

The authors first identify and validate model-agnostic prompt quality merits. They then introduce MePO, a merit-guided, lightweight, and locally deployable prompt optimizer trained on a preference dataset of merit-aligned prompts generated by a lightweight LLM. Unlike previous work, MePO avoids reliance on online optimization, reduces cost and privacy concerns, and generalizes effectively across both large-scale and lightweight models. Experiments demonstrate that MePO achieves better results across diverse tasks and model types.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a fresh perspective on prompt optimization by focusing on interpretable prompt merits and lightweight LLMs. This contrasts with the dominant paradigm of using advanced LLMs as prompt optimizers. The idea of learning from merit-aligned prompts, rather than relying on the output of advanced LLMs directly, is a significant departure. This also ties into the current trend of using LLMs to distill knowledge into smaller models, which increases both performance and efficiency.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant for several reasons:

    *   **Addresses Downward Compatibility:** It tackles the practical issue of prompts generated by powerful LLMs not always being effective for smaller inference models.
    *   **Cost Efficiency:** It offers a more cost-effective and locally deployable alternative to online optimization methods, which rely on expensive API calls.
    *   **Privacy Preservation:** It mitigates privacy concerns associated with sending data to external APIs.
    *   **Generalizability:** MePO's ability to generalize across different model scales and tasks is a valuable feature for real-world deployment.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough empirical validation of its approach, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed prompt merits and the performance of MePO across various datasets and models.
    *   **Interpretable Design:** The focus on interpretable prompt merits makes the approach more transparent and easier to understand, potentially leading to further improvements in prompt design.
    *   **Careful construction of the dataset and the optimization procedure**: the dataset is carefully constructed with both original prompts, and degraded prompts.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of MePO on a range of tasks, it could benefit from evaluation on an even broader set of real-world applications.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvements that are observed can be described as "incremental" as compared to previous approaches.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of prompt engineering by shifting the focus from simply using advanced LLMs to generate prompts to a more nuanced approach that considers prompt merits and model compatibility. MePO's lightweight and locally deployable nature could make it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners working with LLMs in resource-constrained or privacy-sensitive environments.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite incremental improvements, the clear benefits of the MePO framework, including its cost-efficiency, privacy-preserving nature, and consideration of downward compatibility, position it as a notable advancement in the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CartoAgent: a multimodal large language model-powered multi-agent cartographic framework for map style transfer and evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09936v1)
- **Authors**: Chenglong Wang, Yuhao Kang, Zhaoya Gong, Pengjun Zhao, Yu Feng, Wenjia Zhang, Ge Li
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) presents new opportunities to advance the cartographic process. Previous studies have either overlooked the artistic aspects of maps or faced challenges in creating both accurate and informative maps. In this study, we propose CartoAgent, a novel multi-agent cartographic framework powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs). This framework simulates three key stages in cartographic practice: preparation, map design, and evaluation. At each stage, different MLLMs act as agents with distinct roles to collaborate, discuss, and utilize tools for specific purposes. In particular, CartoAgent leverages MLLMs' visual aesthetic capability and world knowledge to generate maps that are both visually appealing and informative. By separating style from geographic data, it can focus on designing stylesheets without modifying the vector-based data, thereby ensuring geographic accuracy. We applied CartoAgent to a specific task centered on map restyling-namely, map style transfer and evaluation. The effectiveness of this framework was validated through extensive experiments and a human evaluation study. CartoAgent can be extended to support a variety of cartographic design decisions and inform future integrations of GenAI in cartography.
