- **Score**: 7/10

### Empirically evaluating commonsense intelligence in large language models with large-scale human judgments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10309v1)
- **Authors**: Tuan Dung Nguyen, Duncan J. Watts, Mark E. Whiting
- **Abstract**: Commonsense intelligence in machines is often assessed by static benchmarks that compare a model's output against human-prescribed correct labels. An important, albeit implicit, assumption of these labels is that they accurately capture what any human would think, effectively treating human common sense as homogeneous. However, recent empirical work has shown that humans vary enormously in what they consider commonsensical; thus what appears self-evident to one benchmark designer may not be so to another. Here, we propose a novel method for evaluating common sense in artificial intelligence (AI), specifically in large language models (LLMs), that incorporates empirically observed heterogeneity among humans by measuring the correspondence between a model's judgment and that of a human population. We first find that, when treated as independent survey respondents, most LLMs remain below the human median in their individual commonsense competence. Second, when used as simulators of a hypothetical population, LLMs correlate with real humans only modestly in the extent to which they agree on the same set of statements. In both cases, smaller, open-weight models are surprisingly more competitive than larger, proprietary frontier models. Our evaluation framework, which ties commonsense intelligence to its cultural basis, contributes to the growing call for adapting AI models to human collectivities that possess different, often incompatible, social stocks of knowledge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Empirically Evaluating Commonsense Intelligence in Large Language Models with Large-Scale Human Judgments" challenges the standard approach of evaluating commonsense in LLMs using static benchmarks with predefined "correct" labels.  The authors argue that this assumes a homogeneity of human commonsense that doesn't exist.  They propose a novel evaluation method that incorporates the empirically observed heterogeneity among humans. They do this by treating LLMs as both independent survey respondents and as simulators of a hypothetical population. By comparing the LLM's "judgments" (expressed as probabilities) against a large dataset of human responses to commonsense statements, they measure the LLM's ability to align with human agreement. The results show that many LLMs perform below the human median and correlate only modestly with real human populations in terms of statement agreement.  Interestingly, smaller, open-weight models often outperform larger, proprietary ones. The authors emphasize that their evaluation framework grounds commonsense intelligence in its cultural basis, advocating for AI models to adapt to diverse human collectives with varying knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a genuinely novel perspective on evaluating commonsense in AI.  The shift from "ground truth" accuracy to alignment with the distribution of human beliefs is a significant departure from traditional benchmarks. The methodology of treating LLMs as both individual survey takers and collective "silicon sample" simulators is creative and insightful.

*   **Significance:** The findings have several significant implications:

    *   **Challenges existing benchmarks:** It directly questions the validity of benchmarks that assume a uniform human understanding of commonsense.

    *   **Highlights heterogeneity:** It underscores the importance of considering the social and cultural basis of commonsense, which is crucial for deploying AI in diverse contexts.

    *   **Democratization of AI:** The observation that smaller, open-weight models can be competitive with larger, proprietary models is a potentially important finding from an accessibility standpoint.

    *   **Focus on Alignment:** Places increased emphasis on *alignment*, not *accuracy* in AI systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Solid Empirical Basis:** The use of a large dataset of human responses provides a strong empirical foundation for the arguments. The experiments are clearly described and well-executed.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The statistical analyses are appropriate, and the authors acknowledge and address potential limitations.
    *   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is well-written, clearly articulating the problem, the proposed solution, and the results. The figures and tables are effective in conveying the key findings.
    *   **Relevance:**  Addresses a core issue of aligning AI with human values and understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Population:**  The human data is primarily from US residents recruited on Amazon Mechanical Turk. This limits the generalizability of the findings to other cultural or demographic groups. The authors acknowledge this, but it's still a significant limitation.
    *   **Choice of Statements:** While the dataset contains a diverse range of statements, it's impossible to guarantee that it fully captures the breadth of human commonsense knowledge. Also, as argued by authors, the dataset is biased by human annotators.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by:

    *   Spurring the development of new, more culturally sensitive and nuanced evaluation metrics for commonsense reasoning.
    *   Encouraging researchers to pay more attention to the social and cultural biases embedded in AI systems.
    *   Driving research into methods for adapting AI models to diverse human collectives.

*   **Additional Comments:** The paper convincingly argues that current LLMs often lack real common sense when judged against the backdrop of human diversity. The most interesting facet of the analysis, is the examination of not just 'accuracy', but also the models' ability to predict human disagreement.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a highly novel and significant contribution to the field of AI evaluation. The conceptual shift from "ground truth" to "alignment" with human distributions is paradigm-shifting. It is not a perfect work, because the sampling of data sets for human judgment needs greater focus, and the study could be further advanced by adding layers of personas onto the LLMs. Despite these limitations, the paper's strengths significantly outweigh its weaknesses, and its potential impact on the field is substantial. The analysis does a good job of showing how LLMs can be very high accuracy, but still not be "common sense". The findings concerning open-weight models are intriguing. The paper has the potential to reshape how we think about evaluating commonsense intelligence in AI.
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### SOS: A Shuffle Order Strategy for Data Augmentation in Industrial Human Activity Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10312v1)
- **Authors**: Anh Tuan Ha, Hoang Khang Phan, Thai Minh Tien Ngo, Anh Phan Truong, Nhat Tan Le
- **Abstract**: In the realm of Human Activity Recognition (HAR), obtaining high quality and variance data is still a persistent challenge due to high costs and the inherent variability of real-world activities. This study introduces a generation dataset by deep learning approaches (Attention Autoencoder and conditional Generative Adversarial Networks). Another problem that data heterogeneity is a critical challenge, one of the solutions is to shuffle the data to homogenize the distribution. Experimental results demonstrate that the random sequence strategy significantly improves classification performance, achieving an accuracy of up to 0.70 $\pm$ 0.03 and a macro F1 score of 0.64 $\pm$ 0.01. For that, disrupting temporal dependencies through random sequence reordering compels the model to focus on instantaneous recognition, thereby improving robustness against activity transitions. This approach not only broadens the effective training dataset but also offers promising avenues for enhancing HAR systems in complex, real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a data augmentation strategy for Human Activity Recognition (HAR) in industrial settings. The key idea is to shuffle the order of time series data segments during training to force the model to focus on instantaneous activity recognition rather than relying on temporal dependencies. The authors combine this shuffling approach with synthetic data generation using Attention Autoencoders (AAEs) and Conditional Generative Adversarial Networks (CTGANs). They evaluate their approach on the OpenPack dataset and show that the random sequence strategy improves classification performance compared to other sequence settings (ascending, real dataset sequence) and baselines, achieving up to 70% accuracy.  The paper compares different combinations of data augmentation techniques (AAE, CTGAN with different sequence settings) and analyzes attention span in the trained models to understand how the different sequence strategies affect the model's learning process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of shuffling time-series data segments for HAR is interesting, but not entirely groundbreaking. Shuffling as a data augmentation technique has been explored in other contexts, but the application to HAR with a focus on industrial activity recognition and comparison with synthetic data generation techniques is a valuable contribution. The combination with AAE and CTGAN for data augmentation adds a layer of complexity and potential benefit.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of the work is moderate. Improving HAR in industrial settings is a relevant problem with practical implications for automation, safety, and workflow optimization. The paper's results demonstrate improvements in classification accuracy, which is a positive step. However, the relatively small margin of improvement over the baseline and the high standard deviation observed in some of the results suggest that the benefits might not always be consistently realized.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined experimental setup.
    *   The paper provides a reasonable justification for the data augmentation technique.
    *   The study analyzes the attention span, offering insight into model learning behaviors.
    *   The paper provides solid baseline model comparison.
    *   The research area is practical and the paper demonstrates tangible improvements in a relevant task.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvement in accuracy is marginal considering the complexity of the model.
    *   The standard deviation reported, suggests inconsistent performance.
    *   The explanation of why shuffling works is somewhat qualitative. A more rigorous analysis, such as examining the frequency components of the signals or quantifying the temporal dependencies in the original data, would strengthen the argument.
    *   The experimental setup compares the method against different synthetic datasets (CTGAN, AAE) but not thoroughly tested with combination strategies.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper has some value, the novelty is moderate, and the experimental results, though positive, show limited and inconsistent improvements. The analysis of attention span offers some insights, but the overall impact on the field is not particularly transformative. There is room to improve upon the reliability and consistency of the results and offer a more thorough explanation of the method.

### J1: Incentivizing Thinking in LLM-as-a-Judge via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10320v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxi Whitehouse, Tianlu Wang, Ping Yu, Xian Li, Jason Weston, Ilia Kulikov, Swarnadeep Saha
- **Abstract**: The progress of AI is bottlenecked by the quality of evaluation, and powerful LLM-as-a-Judge models have proved to be a core solution. Improved judgment ability is enabled by stronger chain-of-thought reasoning, motivating the need to find the best recipes for training such models to think. In this work we introduce J1, a reinforcement learning approach to training such models. Our method converts both verifiable and non-verifiable prompts to judgment tasks with verifiable rewards that incentivize thinking and mitigate judgment bias. In particular, our approach outperforms all other existing 8B or 70B models when trained at those sizes, including models distilled from DeepSeek-R1. J1 also outperforms o1-mini, and even R1 on some benchmarks, despite training a smaller model. We provide analysis and ablations comparing Pairwise-J1 vs Pointwise-J1 models, offline vs online training recipes, reward strategies, seed prompts, and variations in thought length and content. We find that our models make better judgments by learning to outline evaluation criteria, comparing against self-generated reference answers, and re-evaluating the correctness of model responses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "J1: Incentivizing Thinking in LLM-as-a-Judge via Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces J1, a Reinforcement Learning (RL) approach to improve the reasoning abilities of LLM-as-a-Judge models.  J1 converts both verifiable and non-verifiable prompts into judgment tasks with verifiable rewards, encouraging deeper thinking and mitigating judgment biases. The method optimizes both the chain-of-thought reasoning and the final verdict using GRPO.  The authors demonstrate that J1 outperforms several existing 8B and 70B models, including models distilled from DeepSeek-R1, on various reward modeling benchmarks.  They present detailed analyses comparing different J1 variants (Pairwise vs. Pointwise), reward strategies, seed prompts, and thought length/content, highlighting the importance of learning evaluation criteria, comparing against self-generated reference answers, and re-evaluating response correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific RL recipe (J1) for training LLM judges. While using RL for this purpose isn't entirely new (as the paper acknowledges concurrent work), the unique aspects of J1 (particularly, converting both verifiable and non-verifiable prompts to verifiable tasks via synthetic data generation, using GRPO for efficient optimization, and carefully designed consistency-based rewards to mitigate positional bias), distinguishes it from prior work. The analysis of Pointwise vs. Pairwise judges in mitigating position bias is also a valuable contribution. However, the individual components of the J1 recipe (synthetic data, GRPO, consistency rewards) are not completely ground-breaking on their own. The novelty stems more from the careful integration and tuning of these components for the specific task of training LLM judges.

* **Significance:** The significance of the work is in addressing the crucial bottleneck of evaluation quality in AI development.  High-quality LLM judges are essential for training, alignment, and benchmarking.  The demonstrated improvements in judgment accuracy (particularly on tasks requiring complex reasoning, and in mitigating judge bias) suggest that J1 could be a valuable tool for improving the development and evaluation of LLMs. The paper's emphasis on a generalist judge capable of handling both verifiable and non-verifiable tasks is also significant, as it addresses the need for more robust and reliable evaluation across diverse domains. The improvements over distilled models from even larger models (DeepSeek-R1) hint at a significant gain in efficiency and resource utilization. The findings regarding outlining evaluation criteria, generating reference answers, and model re-evaluation patterns also provides valuable insights into desirable judgment behavior for researchers to emulate and investigate in the future.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation across multiple benchmarks, model scales, and J1 variants.
    * **Detailed Analysis:**  The ablations and analyses offer insights into the effectiveness of different design choices.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methods and results.
    * **Practical Implications:** The work has practical implications for improving the evaluation and training of LLMs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** The method's reliance on synthetic training data could limit its ability to generalize to unforeseen scenarios or biases present in real-world human preferences.  Although the authors use WildChat data in their training dataset to alleviate this, further experiments with more diverse real-world data or human preference pairs would strengthen the findings.
    * **Incremental Improvements:** While J1 demonstrates improvements, they are sometimes incremental and may not always close the gap to the very largest models (e.g., DeepSeek-R1 in some cases).
    * **Limited Scope:**  The paper focuses primarily on improving judgment accuracy and mitigating positional bias. Other aspects of LLM judge quality, such as fairness, robustness to adversarial attacks, and cost-effectiveness, are not explored in detail.
    * **Lack of a "Grand Unified Theory":**  While the ablation studies are helpful, the paper doesn't fully articulate *why* the particular combination of synthetic data, GRPO, and consistency rewards work so well.  A deeper theoretical understanding would enhance the paper's impact.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more sophisticated and reliable LLM evaluation methods. The findings on the effectiveness of different RL techniques and reward strategies could guide future research in this area. The specific J1 recipe could be adopted and adapted by other researchers and practitioners to improve their own LLM judges. The comparative study of pointwise and pairwise judges and the advantages of each also presents a promising avenue for future exploration.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed RL recipe (J1) for improving the reasoning abilities of LLM judges, with significant potential to impact the AI development lifecycle. Its comprehensive evaluation and insightful analyses contribute valuable knowledge to the field. While some individual components are based on existing ideas, the specific combination, tuning, and analytical depth of J1 distinguish it. The reliance on synthetic data and the lack of a unifying theoretical explanation are limitations that slightly lower the score. While the work presents incremental improvements over state-of-the-art baselines, these improvements still represent progress in a bottleneck area. The paper justifies a score above 7 with its impact on reasoning in smaller models over its closest competitors, and also its closing the gap or even overtaking larger ones. While falling short of a completely ground-breaking contribution, it provides a substantial advance worthy of strong recognition. The future impact rests on how researchers build upon J1, and also whether J1 is applied to develop and refine smaller or more accessible LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AutoPentest: Enhancing Vulnerability Management With Autonomous LLM Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10321v1)
- **Authors**: Julius Henke
- **Abstract**: A recent area of increasing research is the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in penetration testing, which promises to reduce costs and thus allow for higher frequency. We conduct a review of related work, identifying best practices and common evaluation issues. We then present AutoPentest, an application for performing black-box penetration tests with a high degree of autonomy. AutoPentest is based on the LLM GPT-4o from OpenAI and the LLM agent framework LangChain. It can perform complex multi-step tasks, augmented by external tools and knowledge bases. We conduct a study on three capture-the-flag style Hack The Box (HTB) machines, comparing our implementation AutoPentest with the baseline approach of manually using the ChatGPT-4o user interface. Both approaches are able to complete 15-25 % of the subtasks on the HTB machines, with AutoPentest slightly outperforming ChatGPT. We measure a total cost of \$96.20 US when using AutoPentest across all experiments, while a one-month subscription to ChatGPT Plus costs \$20. The results show that further implementation efforts and the use of more powerful LLMs released in the future are likely to make this a viable part of vulnerability management.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty score and justification.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "AutoPentest," an application designed to automate black-box penetration testing using the GPT-4o language model and the LangChain agent framework. The authors conduct experiments on three Hack The Box (HTB) machines to compare AutoPentest's performance against a baseline approach of manually using ChatGPT-4o. The results indicate that both approaches achieve similar task completion rates (15-25%), with AutoPentest slightly outperforming ChatGPT on one machine. The authors also analyze the monetary cost of using AutoPentest, finding it to be more expensive in terms of direct LLM operation costs than a monthly ChatGPT Plus subscription, but potentially more scalable due to usage limits.  The paper identifies areas for improvement in AutoPentest, such as handling safety concerns, addressing task repetition, and improving memory management, and highlights the potential for future research in areas like white-box penetration testing and evaluation of other LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents an incremental advancement in the application of LLMs to penetration testing.  While several existing works have explored automating aspects of pen testing with LLMs, this paper contributes a specific implementation, AutoPentest, that integrates GPT-4o and LangChain for autonomous black-box testing. The explicit comparison to a manual ChatGPT-4o baseline is a useful contribution, and it acknowledges and builds upon related research. However, the overall approach of using LLM agents for penetration testing is not entirely new, so the novelty is limited to the specific architecture and implementation choices.  A significant drawback is the lack of a more rigorous benchmark and comparison to other automated pentesting tools besides the ChatGPT baseline.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in providing practical insights into the current capabilities and limitations of using LLMs for automated penetration testing. The experiments, although limited in scope (only three HTB machines), offer valuable data on task completion rates and cost considerations. The identification of challenges like task repetition and the need for improved memory management is also a valuable contribution, guiding future research directions.  The authors' responsible approach in terms of ethical considerations and safety concerns when releasing the code publicly further adds to the paper's merit. A key limitation is the relatively low task completion rate (below 30%) across all tested machines, suggesting that the current implementation is far from a fully automated solution. The significance would be higher if the tool achieved a more substantial improvement in task completion.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and research questions.
    *   Well-structured and comprehensive overview of related work.
    *   Detailed description of the AutoPentest architecture and methodology.
    *   Empirical evaluation with comparison to a ChatGPT-4o baseline.
    *   Discussion of ethical considerations and threats to validity.
    *   Public availability of source code.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited scope of experiments (only three HTB machines).
    *   Relatively low task completion rates.
    *   Lack of comparison to a broad range of existing automated pentesting tools.
    *   The "zero-day" argument is questionable given the nature of the evaluation setting.
    *   Limited analysis of the specific vulnerabilities identified and exploited.
    *   Incremental novelty compared to other research in the field.
    *   Lack of comparison to other LLM agent frameworks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by providing a practical example of how to build and evaluate LLM-based automated penetration testing systems. The identified challenges and future research directions can guide further development in this area. However, the limited success rate and incremental novelty may limit the paper's broader impact on the field.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the incremental novelty, the limited scope of experiments, and the relatively low task completion rates, a higher score is not justified. While the paper offers valuable insights and is well-written, it doesn't represent a significant breakthrough in the field. The strengths of the paper are clear, including the careful methodology and open source release.  However, the weaknesses, particularly the limited comparative performance, hold it back from achieving a higher impact. It builds upon existing research and provides useful data, but it doesn't fundamentally change the landscape of automated penetration testing.
