However, medical LLMs tend to exhibit higher toxicity, bias, and potential for harm, impacting their trustworthiness. The authors conclude that there's a trade-off between domain-specific knowledge and safety in health communications, highlighting the need for targeted model design improvements, particularly in mitigating harm and bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a valuable contribution by systematically evaluating a range of LLMs, including both general-purpose and medical-specific models, in the context of cancer communication. While previous studies have assessed LLMs in healthcare, this study's specific focus on breast and cervical cancer communication and the inclusion of multiple dimensions (linguistic quality, safety, and accessibility) enhance its novelty. The comparison between general and medical LLMs is also an insightful aspect, uncovering the trade-off between domain knowledge and safety.
*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications for the development and deployment of AI-driven tools for health communication. By identifying the strengths and weaknesses of different types of LLMs, the study provides guidance for researchers and developers on how to create more effective and safer tools for patient education and support. The emphasis on mitigating harm and bias is particularly crucial in ensuring equitable access to reliable information.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The mixed-methods approach combining quantitative metrics and qualitative expert ratings provides a holistic assessment of LLM performance.
    *   **Focus on Cancer Communication:** The study addresses a significant public health issue and provides valuable insights into how LLMs can be used to improve cancer-related information dissemination.
    *   **Comparison of LLM Types:** The comparison between general-purpose and medical LLMs reveals important trade-offs and guides future development efforts.
    *   **Statistical Rigor:** The use of appropriate statistical tests (Welch's ANOVA, Games-Howell post hoc, Hedges' g) adds credibility to the quantitative findings.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** The study relies on a curated dataset from publicly available medical datasets. The dataset and resulting analysis, should be interpreted with consideration of its limitations. As the models continue to evolve, datasets should be frequently updated to reflect newer data.
    *   **Generalizability:** The results may not be directly generalizable to all proprietary LLMs or real-world patient interactions.
    *   **Contextual Specificity**: The assessment may be confined by assumptions of the cultural and linguistic context in which the LLMs were tested, which might not translate to other contexts. This aspect should be addressed more robustly, especially when addressing equity issues.
    *   **Racial Bias Measurement**: While the study measures racial bias, the methodology of using "in-context impersonation" is a useful start, but needs more complex analyses and justification.
*   **Potential Influence:** The study has the potential to influence the design and development of future AI-driven health communication tools. The findings highlight the importance of incorporating safety measures, mitigating bias, and ensuring accessibility when using LLMs to generate health-related information. The study could also encourage further research into hybrid approaches that combine the strengths of general-purpose and medical LLMs.
*   **Room for Improvement**: The evaluation methodology can be improved by incorporating larger and more diverse patient datasets. The evaluation could be significantly strengthened by including a real-world user testing phase, especially with diverse user groups, to gain valuable insights into the actual impact of the generated content on knowledge, behavior, and emotional well-being.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The study makes a significant contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs for cancer communication. Its focus on multiple dimensions (linguistic quality, safety, and accessibility) and comparison of general and medical LLMs are particularly valuable. The findings have important implications for the design and development of AI-driven tools for health communication. However, the study is limited by the dataset, assumptions in the measurement of cultural bias, and lack of real-world validation, preventing it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fine-tuning Diffusion Policies with Backpropagation Through Diffusion Timesteps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10482v1)
- **Authors**: Ningyuan Yang, Jiaxuan Gao, Feng Gao, Yi Wu, Chao Yu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion policies, widely adopted in decision-making scenarios such as robotics, gaming and autonomous driving, are capable of learning diverse skills from demonstration data due to their high representation power. However, the sub-optimal and limited coverage of demonstration data could lead to diffusion policies that generate sub-optimal trajectories and even catastrophic failures. While reinforcement learning (RL)-based fine-tuning has emerged as a promising solution to address these limitations, existing approaches struggle to effectively adapt Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to diffusion models. This challenge stems from the computational intractability of action likelihood estimation during the denoising process, which leads to complicated optimization objectives. In our experiments starting from randomly initialized policies, we find that online tuning of Diffusion Policies demonstrates much lower sample efficiency compared to directly applying PPO on MLP policies (MLP+PPO). To address these challenges, we introduce NCDPO, a novel framework that reformulates Diffusion Policy as a noise-conditioned deterministic policy. By treating each denoising step as a differentiable transformation conditioned on pre-sampled noise, NCDPO enables tractable likelihood evaluation and gradient backpropagation through all diffusion timesteps. Our experiments demonstrate that NCDPO achieves sample efficiency comparable to MLP+PPO when training from scratch, outperforming existing methods in both sample efficiency and final performance across diverse benchmarks, including continuous robot control and multi-agent game scenarios. Furthermore, our experimental results show that our method is robust to the number denoising timesteps in the Diffusion Policy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Fine-tuning Diffusion Policies with Backpropagation Through Diffusion Timesteps":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of fine-tuning diffusion policies for decision-making tasks using reinforcement learning (RL). Diffusion policies, while powerful, can generate sub-optimal trajectories when trained solely on limited demonstration data. Fine-tuning them with RL is a natural solution, but existing methods like DPPO (Diffusion Policy Policy Optimization) suffer from poor sample efficiency due to the difficulty of estimating action likelihoods during the diffusion denoising process. This difficulty effectively enlarges the action space for the RL algorithm, hindering learning.

The authors propose a novel framework called Noise-Conditioned Diffusion Policy Optimization (NCDPO). NCDPO reformulates the diffusion policy as a noise-conditioned deterministic policy.  Instead of optimizing over the Gaussian likelihood of all denoising steps, NCDPO leverages Backpropagation through Diffusion Timesteps (BPDT).  It treats each denoising step as a deterministic transformation conditioned on pre-sampled noise, allowing for tractable likelihood evaluation of actions. This simplifies the RL objective, making it more sample efficient. The authors demonstrate through experiments that NCDPO achieves sample efficiency comparable to directly applying PPO to MLP policies (MLP+PPO) and outperforms existing methods in various benchmarks, including continuous robot control and multi-agent game scenarios.  Ablation studies also show NCDPO is robust to the number of diffusion timesteps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of reframing the diffusion policy denoising process as a deterministic, noise-conditioned process and applying backpropagation through the timesteps is novel. Existing methods struggle with estimating the action likelihood during denoising, which complicates the RL objective and increases the effective action space. NCDPO avoids this by directly training the deterministic denoising process. The connection to and derivation of deterministic transformations conditioned on pre-sampled noise ensures tractable likelihood evaluation.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant bottleneck in applying RL to fine-tune diffusion policies. The sample efficiency problem of DPPO is a practical hurdle, and NCDPO provides a viable solution. The experimental results on robot control and multi-agent environments clearly demonstrate NCDPO's improved performance over existing methods, showcasing its potential to advance the field of diffusion policy learning. The robustness to diffusion timesteps is also a valuable finding, suggesting the method is not overly sensitive to a hyperparameter.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the issue of sample inefficiency in DPPO and motivates the need for a better fine-tuning approach.
    *   **Well-defined method:** NCDPO is presented with sufficient detail and mathematical justification, making the approach relatively easy to understand and implement.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The experiments across different environments and comparisons to strong baselines provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of NCDPO.
    *   **Ablation studies:** The ablation studies on denoising timesteps and initial noise scaling demonstrate the robustness and guide future use.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While significant, it can be argued the method is an incremental improvement over DPPO since it still uses PPO as the base RL algorithm. This may cause some to view the contribution as less groundbreaking.
    *   **Limited Real-World Evaluation:** The paper does not include any real-world robot experiments, which would further strengthen the practical impact of the research. Sim-to-real transfer is a known challenge, and demonstrating NCDPO's performance in a physical setting would be invaluable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field in several ways:

    *   **New RL-Finte-tuning Approach:** It provides a more efficient and practical way to fine-tune diffusion policies using RL, making it easier to leverage the benefits of both approaches.
    *   **Deterministic Reparameterization:** The noise-conditioned deterministic policy framework could inspire other research in diffusion-based learning.
    *   **Broad Application:** NCDPO's ability to handle both continuous and discrete action spaces makes it a versatile tool for a wide range of decision-making problems.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the NCDPO framework, the clear improvement in sample efficiency compared to existing methods, the strong experimental validation across multiple environments, and the potential for influencing future research in diffusion policy learning, I assign a score of 8. The paper addresses a relevant and important problem, offers a well-designed solution, and provides convincing empirical results. While the approach is an incremental improvement of existing methods and lacks real-world robotic evaluation, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, making this a significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Campus AI vs Commercial AI: A Late-Breaking Study on How LLM As-A-Service Customizations Shape Trust and Usage Patterns
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10490v1)
- **Authors**: Leon Hannig, Annika Bush, Meltem Aksoy, Steffen Becker, Greta Ontrup
- **Abstract**: As the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) by students, lecturers and researchers becomes more prevalent, universities - like other organizations - are pressed to develop coherent AI strategies. LLMs as-a-Service (LLMaaS) offer accessible pre-trained models, customizable to specific (business) needs. While most studies prioritize data, model, or infrastructure adaptations (e.g., model fine-tuning), we focus on user-salient customizations, like interface changes and corporate branding, which we argue influence users' trust and usage patterns. This study serves as a functional prequel to a large-scale field study in which we examine how students and employees at a German university perceive and use their institution's customized LLMaaS compared to ChatGPT. The goals of this prequel are to stimulate discussions on psychological effects of LLMaaS customizations and refine our research approach through feedback. Our forthcoming findings will deepen the understanding of trust dynamics in LLMs, providing practical guidance for organizations considering LLMaaS deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores how customizations of Large Language Models as-a-Service (LLMaaS) influence user trust, perception, and usage patterns within a university setting, compared to commercially available LLMs like ChatGPT. It argues that even seemingly superficial customizations (like branding) can impact user perception and therefore, trust and usage.  It outlines a large-scale field study that aims to investigate these effects, framing it as a "prequel" to stimulate discussion and refine their research approach. Hypotheses are presented regarding the effects of LLMaaS customizations on trust, hallucination detection, privacy perceptions, and sustainable AI use.  The paper describes the planned methodology, including the study design, participant pool, and survey instrument used to gather data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant and Timely Topic:**  The paper tackles a crucial issue as organizations, especially universities, grapple with integrating LLMs. Understanding the impact of different implementation strategies on user perception is critical.
*   **Focus on User-Salient Customizations:** The focus on user-salient customizations (UI changes, branding, warnings) is a novel angle. Much of the existing research emphasizes technical aspects like model fine-tuning. This paper rightly points out that even non-technical customizations could significantly influence users.
*   **Well-Defined Hypotheses:** The hypotheses are clearly articulated and grounded in previous research on trust, AI perception, and related areas.
*   **Sound Methodology:**  The planned methodology (large-scale field study with a comprehensive survey) seems appropriate for addressing the research question.  The inclusion of validated measures enhances the reliability of the findings.
*   **Practical Implications:** The research has clear practical implications for organizations deploying LLMaaS, providing insights into how to customize these systems to foster appropriate trust and usage.
*   **CSR Considerations:** Integrating the concept of corporate social responsibility (CSR) and environmental impact (sustainable use) is a welcome addition and reflects the growing awareness in the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **"Prequel" Nature:** The paper is essentially a research proposal/design paper.  There are no results or data presented, which limits its immediate impact. The value lies in the potential of the planned study.
*   **Limited Novelty in Hypotheses:**  While the focus on user-salient customizations is interesting, some of the hypotheses (e.g., H1: higher trust in customized LLMaaS, H5: greater perceived privacy) are somewhat predictable.  The strength will depend on the nuance and magnitude of the effects revealed by the data.
*   **Potential for Confounding Variables:** Field studies in real-world environments are inherently susceptible to confounding variables.  For example, prior user experiences with ChatGPT, varying levels of AI literacy, and individual differences in trust propensity could influence the results. The paper doesn't explicitly address how these will be controlled for or accounted for in the analysis.
*   **Generalizability:**  The study is planned within a single German university.  The generalizability of the findings to other contexts (different types of organizations, cultures) needs to be considered.
*   **Overclaims:** The paper claims that "customization choices could serve as salient trust cues for users." A causal relationship cannot be asserted.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to a growing body of research on human-AI interaction and the responsible adoption of LLMs. Its focus on user-salient customizations addresses a relatively under-explored area. If the planned study yields significant and nuanced results, it could provide valuable guidance to organizations deploying LLMaaS. It also presents new research directions for investigating how customisation aspects varies across sectors and long-term impacts on organizational culture.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**.

*   The paper addresses a *relevant* and *timely* research question with a *useful* methodology. The planned study has the *potential* to contribute valuable insights to the field.
*   However, it lacks *empirical results*, and there is an absence of a *testable experiment*, therefore making the paper largely a *design paper*, weakening its direct contribution. The hypotheses, while grounded in literature, are not particularly groundbreaking. The study's *generalizability* is a concern. Furthermore, there are questions on what variables they will control and questions on what they hope to achieve.
*   Overall, the paper is a *solid foundation for further research*, but its novelty and significance are currently limited by its *lack of empirical validation*. The value lies in the potential impact of the planned study.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### CL-RAG: Bridging the Gap in Retrieval-Augmented Generation with Curriculum Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10493v1)
- **Authors**: Shaohan Wang, Licheng Zhang, Zheren Fu, Zhendong Mao
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an effective method to enhance the capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Existing methods focus on optimizing the retriever or generator in the RAG system by directly utilizing the top-k retrieved documents. However, the documents effectiveness are various significantly across user queries, i.e. some documents provide valuable knowledge while others totally lack critical information. It hinders the retriever and generator's adaptation during training. Inspired by human cognitive learning, curriculum learning trains models using samples progressing from easy to difficult, thus enhancing their generalization ability, and we integrate this effective paradigm to the training of the RAG system. In this paper, we propose a multi-stage Curriculum Learning based RAG system training framework, named CL-RAG. We first construct training data with multiple difficulty levels for the retriever and generator separately through sample evolution. Then, we train the model in stages based on the curriculum learning approach, thereby optimizing the overall performance and generalization of the RAG system more effectively. Our CL-RAG framework demonstrates consistent effectiveness across four open-domain QA datasets, achieving performance gains of 2% to 4% over multiple advanced methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CL-RAG, a curriculum learning-based training framework for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. The core idea is to improve RAG performance by training the retriever and generator in stages, starting with simpler examples and progressively introducing more complex ones, mimicking human cognitive learning. The authors construct training data with varying difficulty levels for both the retriever and the generator through sample evolution techniques (rewriting queries and using counterfactual examples). The framework involves finetuning a Retrieval-Augmented Language Model (RALM) stage-by-stage based on the difficulty of the documents and using a well-trained RALM to assess document quality for retriever training. Experiments across four open-domain question-answering datasets demonstrate consistent performance gains (2-4%) compared to several advanced baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of curriculum learning to RAG systems. While curriculum learning is a well-established technique in machine learning, its integration within the RAG framework, particularly with the specific strategies for constructing difficulty levels for both retriever and generator, is a valuable contribution. The approach of using the *trained* RALM to evaluate and rerank documents for retriever training (instead of a raw LLM) is also a notable refinement.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in addressing a crucial limitation of standard RAG training, which often neglects the varying quality of retrieved documents. By explicitly considering document difficulty, the CL-RAG framework enhances the model's ability to learn from diverse information and counteract distracting noise. The consistent performance improvements across multiple datasets suggest the framework's general applicability and potential for widespread adoption. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each training stage, making the paper stronger.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed curriculum learning framework with specific strategies for creating data with varying difficulty levels for both retriever and generator.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and baselines.
    *   Ablation studies that provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   The idea of using the RALM itself for reranking/assessing documents for retrieval, which is a reasonable way to avoid unstable raw LLM assessments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The complexity of data construction might be a barrier to entry for some researchers. While the paper describes the process, replicating it might require substantial effort.
    *   The paper would benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the types of questions or scenarios where CL-RAG provides the most significant advantage. Are there specific knowledge domains or question complexities where the curriculum learning approach is particularly effective?
    *   The limitations section points out that the current model only has a single iteration of training. This could be developed for future work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in RAG systems by highlighting the importance of considering document difficulty during training. It provides a solid foundation for developing more sophisticated curriculum learning strategies for RAG, potentially leading to even better performance and robustness. This will likely be extended to other approaches and datasets within the field.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a novel and useful contribution to the field of RAG systems by adapting curriculum learning techniques. The approach is sound, well-motivated, and supported by strong experimental results. While the data construction complexity could be reduced and further analysis of the framework's strengths in specific scenarios is warranted, the overall impact and potential influence justify a strong rating.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can You Really Trust Code Copilots? Evaluating Large Language Models from a Code Security Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10494v1)
- **Authors**: Yutao Mou, Xiao Deng, Yuxiao Luo, Shikun Zhang, Wei Ye
- **Abstract**: Code security and usability are both essential for various coding assistant applications driven by large language models (LLMs). Current code security benchmarks focus solely on single evaluation task and paradigm, such as code completion and generation, lacking comprehensive assessment across dimensions like secure code generation, vulnerability repair and discrimination. In this paper, we first propose CoV-Eval, a multi-task benchmark covering various tasks such as code completion, vulnerability repair, vulnerability detection and classification, for comprehensive evaluation of LLM code security. Besides, we developed VC-Judge, an improved judgment model that aligns closely with human experts and can review LLM-generated programs for vulnerabilities in a more efficient and reliable way. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 20 proprietary and open-source LLMs. Overall, while most LLMs identify vulnerable codes well, they still tend to generate insecure codes and struggle with recognizing specific vulnerability types and performing repairs. Extensive experiments and qualitative analyses reveal key challenges and optimization directions, offering insights for future research in LLM code security.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CoV-Eval, a multi-task benchmark for evaluating the code security of Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks that focus on single tasks (e.g., code completion or generation) by offering a comprehensive evaluation across secure code generation, vulnerability repair, detection, and classification.  The benchmark covers 18 vulnerability types in different programming languages.  The authors also developed VC-Judge, an improved judgment model designed to align more closely with human experts in identifying vulnerabilities.  They evaluate 20 proprietary and open-source LLMs using CoV-Eval, revealing that while LLMs can often identify vulnerable code, they still tend to generate insecure code and struggle with specific vulnerability types and repair tasks.  The paper includes extensive experiments, qualitative analyses, and discussions of optimization directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Comprehensive Benchmark:** The paper fills a crucial gap by providing a comprehensive, multi-task benchmark for code security in LLMs. This is a significant improvement over single-task evaluations and allows for a more holistic understanding of LLM security capabilities. CoV-Eval provides diverse perspectives, including secure code generation, vulnerability repair, and discrimination capabilities of LLMs.
    * **Improved Evaluation Methodology:** VC-Judge addresses the limitations of traditional static analysis tools and earlier LLM-based evaluations by improving accuracy and reliability in identifying vulnerabilities.
    * **Extensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is thorough, covering a wide range of both proprietary and open-source LLMs, allowing for meaningful comparisons and insights.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a real-world concern, as LLMs are increasingly used in coding assistance applications. Evaluating code security is vital for preventing malicious code generation or information leakage.
    * **Vul-Evol Framework**: The developed Vul-Evol synthesis framework to generate more complex code scenarios is a promising feature to enhance the diversity of evaluation datasets and provide data for improving code security of LLMs.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Dataset Limitations:** The Vul-Evol framework relies on synthesizing new scenarios based on an initial seed set. While the authors claim it creates more complex scenarios, the diversity of the synthesized scenarios might still be limited by the characteristics of the seed set.  The paper acknowledges this, stating plans to incorporate more diverse code scenarios in the future.
    * **Evaluation Metrics:** While the "Security Rate (SR)" is a useful metric, the paper relies heavily on regular matching for the detection and classification tasks. This approach might be too simplistic and may not capture the nuances of LLM responses.  A more sophisticated semantic analysis could improve the reliability of evaluation.
    * **Limited Repair Capabilities:** Results indicate LLMs still have limitations in vulnerability repair even when vulnerability descriptions are provided. Future studies may want to investigate why vulnerability repair is so difficult and how better prompt engineering or training data can be used to achieve better results.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

    * The multi-task approach and improved evaluation method are novel aspects, representing a substantial advancement in the evaluation of LLM code security.
    * The paper's findings have significant implications for the development and deployment of secure coding assistants and highlight crucial challenges that must be addressed to build more trustworthy systems.
    * The detailed analysis provides valuable insights for future research, guiding optimization efforts to improve the security of LLMs.

* **Potential Influence:**

    * This benchmark is likely to become a standard tool for evaluating LLM code security.
    * The findings will inform the development of more secure coding assistants, potentially impacting software development practices.
    *  The paper will stimulate further research into the challenges of secure code generation and vulnerability repair in LLMs.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper makes a strong contribution to the field by providing a much-needed comprehensive benchmark for evaluating LLM code security. The creation of CoV-Eval and VC-Judge represents a substantial improvement in the evaluation methodology. The extensive experiments and analyses offer valuable insights and guidance for future research. The real-world relevance of the topic further enhances its significance. The paper's primary weakness lies in the reliance of Vul-Evol on a seed set. Therefore, there's room for improvement in terms of dataset diversity, and more sophisticated evaluation metrics could be considered. Nevertheless, the paper's strengths outweigh these weaknesses, making it a significant contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### RouteNator: A Router-Based Multi-Modal Architecture for Generating Synthetic Training Data for Function Calling LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10495v1)
- **Authors**: Vibha Belavadi, Tushar Vatsa, Dewang Sultania, Suhas Suresha, Ishita Verma, Cheng Chen, Tracy Holloway King, Michael Friedrich
- **Abstract**: This paper addresses fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) for function calling tasks when real user interaction data is unavailable. In digital content creation tools, where users express their needs through natural language queries that must be mapped to API calls, the lack of real-world task-specific data and privacy constraints for training on it necessitate synthetic data generation. Existing approaches to synthetic data generation fall short in diversity and complexity, failing to replicate real-world data distributions and leading to suboptimal performance after LLM fine-tuning. We present a novel router-based architecture that leverages domain resources like content metadata and structured knowledge graphs, along with text-to-text and vision-to-text language models to generate high-quality synthetic training data. Our architecture's flexible routing mechanism enables synthetic data generation that matches observed real-world distributions, addressing a fundamental limitation of traditional approaches. Evaluation on a comprehensive set of real user queries demonstrates significant improvements in both function classification accuracy and API parameter selection. Models fine-tuned with our synthetic data consistently outperform traditional approaches, establishing new benchmarks for function calling tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "RouteNator: A Router-Based Multi-Modal Architecture for Generating Synthetic Training Data for Function Calling LLMs" addresses the challenge of fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) for function calling tasks when real-world user interaction data is scarce or unavailable due to privacy constraints. The authors propose a novel router-based architecture that leverages domain resources like content metadata, knowledge graphs, and both text-to-text and vision-to-text language models to generate high-quality synthetic training data. The key innovation is a routing mechanism that directs query generation requests to appropriate prompt templates based on population-level statistics. The architecture includes multiple specialized prompt templates, a weighted routing mechanism, and integration of multi-modal language models to increase data diversity. The authors demonstrate that their approach leads to significant improvements in both function classification accuracy and API parameter selection compared to traditional synthetic data generation methods. They evaluate their system using a comprehensive set of real user queries and show consistent outperformance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a well-integrated architecture that demonstrates a clear advantage over existing synthetic data generation methods for function calling. The novelty lies in the specific combination of several techniques: the router-based approach guided by population-level statistics, the integration of both text-to-text and vision-to-text models, and the use of domain-specific knowledge graphs and content metadata. This combination addresses the limitations of simpler rule-based or single-prompt LLM generation methods. However, the individual components are not entirely novel in isolation. For example, instruction tuning and knowledge graph utilization are established techniques. The novelty comes from the specific architecture and how these techniques are combined and refined for the particular problem of function calling data generation.

*   **Significance:** Function calling is an increasingly important capability for LLMs in real-world applications, especially in digital content creation. The lack of real-world training data for this task poses a significant problem. This paper offers a practical solution to generate realistic and diverse synthetic data that improves the performance of function calling LLMs. The empirical results presented in the paper are convincing, demonstrating a clear improvement in function classification accuracy and API parameter selection. The paper's emphasis on matching real-world data distributions in synthetic data generation is a valuable contribution. This is particularly important since biases in training datasets can lead to poor performance in the real world. However, it is not immediately clear whether the synthetic data is as good as real-world data. A comparison with model trained using real world data would further improve the significance of the findings.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies a practical problem and proposes a well-designed solution.
    *   The router-based architecture is a novel and effective approach for generating high-quality synthetic data.
    *   The integration of multi-modal language models increases data diversity.
    *   The paper provides comprehensive experimental results that demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   The paper discusses practical implementation details.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the overall architecture is novel, the individual components (LLMs, knowledge graphs, etc.) are not entirely new.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed comparison to other state-of-the-art synthetic data generation methods for similar tasks.
    *   The performance on real-world dataset is not explored.
    *   It is not discussed how the model performs on edge cases where content metadata and knowledge graphs are not rich in information.
    *   The dependence on specific domain-specific resources (knowledge graphs, metadata) might limit the generalizability of the approach to other domains without such resources.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of function calling LLMs, especially in domains where real-world data is limited. The proposed architecture can be used to generate realistic and diverse training data, improving the accuracy and reliability of function calling models. The insights on balancing synthetic data distribution and query characteristics contribute to a better understanding of training LLMs for this task. It is not very easy to replicate this research, since it heavily depends on domain specific knowledge graph and metadata.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of function calling LLMs by introducing a novel and effective architecture for synthetic data generation. While the individual components are not entirely new, the specific combination and refinement of these techniques in the router-based multi-modal architecture is significant. The paper demonstrates clear performance improvements over traditional approaches and provides valuable insights into training LLMs for this task.

The paper's impact is limited by its dependence on domain-specific resources and the lack of real world performance metrics. Although the approach relies on publicly available models for text to text and vision to text transformations, it can be improved by using a more general approach that depends less on domain-specific resources. Also a thorough comparison with real-world datasets should validate the research even further.

While the approach may not revolutionize the field, it provides a practical and effective solution to a pressing problem and has the potential to influence the development of function calling LLMs in various domains.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### S3C2 Summit 2024-09: Industry Secure Software Supply Chain Summit
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10538v1)
- **Authors**: Imranur Rahman, Yasemin Acar, Michel Cukier, William Enck, Christian Kastner, Alexandros Kapravelos, Dominik Wermke, Laurie Williams
- **Abstract**: While providing economic and software development value, software supply chains are only as strong as their weakest link. Over the past several years, there has been an exponential increase in cyberattacks, specifically targeting vulnerable links in critical software supply chains. These attacks disrupt the day-to-day functioning and threaten the security of nearly everyone on the internet, from billion-dollar companies and government agencies to hobbyist open-source developers. The ever-evolving threat of software supply chain attacks has garnered interest from the software industry and the US government in improving software supply chain security. On September 20, 2024, three researchers from the NSF-backed Secure Software Supply Chain Center (S3C2) conducted a Secure Software Supply Chain Summit with a diverse set of 12 practitioners from 9 companies. The goals of the Summit were to: (1) to enable sharing between individuals from different companies regarding practical experiences and challenges with software supply chain security, (2) to help form new collaborations, (3) to share our observations from our previous summits with industry, and (4) to learn about practitioners' challenges to inform our future research direction. The summit consisted of discussions of six topics relevant to the companies represented, including updating vulnerable dependencies, component and container choice, malicious commits, building infrastructure, large language models, and reducing entire classes of vulnerabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper summarizes the key discussions and findings from a Secure Software Supply Chain (S3C2) Summit held in September 2024. The summit brought together industry practitioners to discuss challenges and best practices related to securing software supply chains. The paper covers six main topics: updating vulnerable dependencies, component and container choice, malicious commits, build infrastructure, large language models (LLMs) and supply chain security, and reducing entire classes of vulnerabilities. For each topic, the paper outlines the current state of practice, identifies open questions, and highlights key takeaways from the discussions. The summit operated under Chatham House Rule, ensuring candid conversations. The paper aims to share these insights and inform future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary value of this paper lies in its documentation of current industry practices and identified challenges, rather than in presenting novel technical solutions. While the topics discussed are highly relevant, the paper mainly synthesizes perspectives and experiences from practitioners. The novelty is limited to the specific collection of insights gathered during this summit, offering a snapshot in time.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Practical Insights:** It provides a real-world view of the challenges faced by companies in securing their software supply chains. This is valuable for researchers and practitioners alike.
    *   **Open Questions:** It highlights open research questions that need to be addressed to improve software supply chain security. This can help guide future research efforts.
    *   **Industry Trends:** It documents the increasing adoption of LLMs in software development and the associated security concerns. It also touches on trends like the move towards memory-safe languages and the challenges of reproducible builds.
    *   **Focus on Practicality:** The paper emphasizes practical considerations and actionable strategies, making it relevant for industry professionals seeking to improve their security posture.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Real-world Perspective:** The paper captures the perspectives of practitioners, providing insights into the challenges and trade-offs they face.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** It covers a wide range of topics related to software supply chain security.
    *   **Well-organized:** The paper is well-structured, with clear sections and summaries.
    *   **Identifies Open Questions:** The paper explicitly states open questions that could inform further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Depth:** Given that it's a summary of a summit, the paper doesn't delve deeply into any one topic.
    *   **Descriptive rather than Prescriptive:** It mainly describes the current state and challenges, without providing specific solutions or recommendations.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The findings may not be generalizable to all organizations, as they are based on the experiences of a specific group of participants.
    *   **Time-Sensitive:** Given the rapid evolution of the field, some of the observations may become outdated relatively quickly.
    *   **Unclear Selection Criteria:** The selection process of the 12 participants is not specified. This means that the generalizability of the study is hard to assess.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research directions and inform industry best practices. By highlighting the key challenges and open questions, it can guide researchers toward relevant areas of investigation.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

A score of 6 reflects the paper's moderate novelty and significance. While it does not present groundbreaking technical solutions, it makes a valuable contribution by documenting current industry practices and identifying open challenges in securing software supply chains. The paper's strengths lie in its real-world perspective, comprehensive coverage, and clear organization. However, its weaknesses include a lack of depth, its descriptive rather than prescriptive nature, limited generalizability, and time-sensitivity. The emphasis is on the practical concerns and challenges of practitioners, making it a valuable summary of the state of the field in the face of very fast change, but limiting the novelty.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Exploring Implicit Visual Misunderstandings in Multimodal Large Language Models through Attention Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10541v1)
- **Authors**: Pengfei Wang, Guohai Xu, Weinong Wang, Junjie Yang, Jie Lou, Yunhua Xue
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements have enhanced the capability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to comprehend multi-image information. However, existing benchmarks primarily evaluate answer correctness, overlooking whether models genuinely comprehend the visual input. To address this, we define implicit visual misunderstanding (IVM), where MLLMs provide correct answers without fully comprehending the visual input. Through our analysis, we decouple the visual and textual modalities within the causal attention module, revealing that attention distribution increasingly converges on the image associated with the correct answer as the network layers deepen. This insight leads to the introduction of a scale-agnostic metric, \textit{attention accuracy}, and a novel benchmark for quantifying IVMs. Attention accuracy directly evaluates the model's visual understanding via internal mechanisms, remaining robust to positional biases for more reliable assessments. Furthermore, we extend our approach to finer granularities and demonstrate its effectiveness in unimodal scenarios, underscoring its versatility and generalizability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Exploring Implicit Visual Misunderstandings in Multimodal Large Language Models through Attention Analysis":

**Summary:**

This paper addresses a critical but often overlooked aspect of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs):  implicit visual misunderstandings (IVMs).  The authors define IVMs as situations where an MLLM provides the correct answer to a multi-image task, but its internal reasoning and attention mechanisms indicate a misunderstanding of the visual input. The paper introduces a methodology to decouple visual and textual modalities within the causal attention module of MLLMs, revealing that attention distribution converges on the "correct" image as network layers deepen. Based on this finding, they propose a scale-agnostic metric called "attention accuracy" and a new benchmark (STME) to quantify IVMs. The authors demonstrate that attention accuracy offers a more reliable and robust evaluation of a model's true visual understanding, independent of positional biases. They further extend their approach to finer granularities and unimodal scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in:

*   **Defining and Formalizing IVMs:**  While previous work focused on answer correctness, this paper identifies and names the critical problem of implicit misunderstanding, shifting the focus from simply *what* answer to *why* the model arrives at that answer and *if* the answer is derived through appropriate and correct visual processing.
*   **Attention-Based Analysis:** Using attention analysis to probe the internal reasoning of MLLMs is not entirely new, but the specific application to uncover visual misunderstandings and quantify them is a novel approach. The method to decouple visual and textual modalities within the attention module is a strong point.
*   **Attention Accuracy Metric:** While the concept of using attention weights to evaluate the model has merit, attention weights themselves can sometimes be misleading and require careful interpretation. The metric aims to be scale-agnostic but the actual effectiveness of this depends on the thoroughness of experiments that address scalability.
*   **STME Benchmark:** Creating a dataset tailored to expose IVMs is a valuable contribution. The dataset includes a diverse set of visual tasks with varying difficulty levels.
*   **Generalizability:** Applying the analysis to both multi-image and unimodal scenarios, along with the use of scale-agnostic metrics, adds to the novelty and potential generalizability of the approach.

**Significance:**

The work has significant implications:

*   **Improved Evaluation:**  It provides a more nuanced way to evaluate MLLMs, going beyond simple accuracy and giving insight into actual visual understanding.
*   **Model Debugging and Improvement:** By identifying IVMs, developers can pinpoint areas where models need improvement in their visual processing abilities and/or dataset biases that favor incorrect reasoning.
*   **Trustworthiness:** Addressing IVMs is essential for building more reliable and trustworthy MLLMs. A model giving the correct answer for the wrong reasons can lead to unexpected failures and safety concerns in real-world applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of IVMs and its importance.
*   **Thorough Analysis:** The attention-based analysis is well-explained and supported by experimental results.
*   **Benchmark and Metric:** The STME benchmark and attention accuracy metric are valuable tools for evaluating and comparing MLLMs.
*   **Clear and Concise Writing:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to understand.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Attention Mechanisms:** Attention mechanisms are known to sometimes not accurately reflect the "true" reasoning of a model. While a useful proxy, reliance solely on attention might provide an incomplete picture.
*   **Complexity of Implementation:** The method requires access and modification of the internal structure of the MLLM, which might not be feasible for all users, especially those working with proprietary models.
*   **Limited Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper effectively diagnoses IVMs, it provides limited guidance on how to *mitigate* them. Further research should focus on this aspect.
*   **Dataset Limitations:** While the STME dataset is a strong start, it may not be comprehensive enough to cover all potential scenarios where IVMs could occur.

**Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to influence research in MLLM evaluation, training, and interpretability. It highlights a crucial aspect of model behavior that has been largely ignored, and it provides concrete tools for addressing this issue.  Future research can build upon this work by exploring mitigation strategies, developing more sophisticated attention-based analysis techniques, and creating more comprehensive benchmarks.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper's contribution is significant due to the formalization of IVMs and the introduction of a methodology for their detection.  The introduction of a custom dataset is commendable. While not perfectly revolutionary, this paper moves the field towards more robust understanding of MLLMs. However, the dependence on attention mechanisms (which are known to be imperfect reflections of a model's reasoning), coupled with the practical difficulties of implementing the method and the limited exploration of mitigation strategies, prevents a higher score. The work's impact depends largely on its ability to stimulate further research in this direction and to provide tangible benefits to the MLLM community. Given the identified strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 reflects a solid contribution with clear potential for future impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards a Deeper Understanding of Reasoning Capabilities in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10543v1)
- **Authors**: Annie Wong, Thomas Bäck, Aske Plaat, Niki van Stein, Anna V. Kononova
- **Abstract**: While large language models demonstrate impressive performance on static benchmarks, the true potential of large language models as self-learning and reasoning agents in dynamic environments remains unclear. This study systematically evaluates the efficacy of self-reflection, heuristic mutation, and planning as prompting techniques to test the adaptive capabilities of agents. We conduct experiments with various open-source language models in dynamic environments and find that larger models generally outperform smaller ones, but that strategic prompting can close this performance gap. Second, a too-long prompt can negatively impact smaller models on basic reactive tasks, while larger models show more robust behaviour. Third, advanced prompting techniques primarily benefit smaller models on complex games, but offer less improvement for already high-performing large language models. Yet, we find that advanced reasoning methods yield highly variable outcomes: while capable of significantly improving performance when reasoning and decision-making align, they also introduce instability and can lead to big performance drops. Compared to human performance, our findings reveal little evidence of true emergent reasoning. Instead, large language model performance exhibits persistent limitations in crucial areas such as planning, reasoning, and spatial coordination, suggesting that current-generation large language models still suffer fundamental shortcomings that may not be fully overcome through self-reflective prompting alone. Reasoning is a multi-faceted task, and while reasoning methods like Chain of thought improves multi-step reasoning on math word problems, our findings using dynamic benchmarks highlight important shortcomings in general reasoning capabilities, indicating a need to move beyond static benchmarks to capture the complexity of reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the reasoning capabilities of open-source Large Language Models (LLMs) in dynamic environments using the SmartPlay benchmark. It examines the efficacy of self-reflection, heuristic mutation (using an "Oracle"), and planning as prompting techniques across various challenge levels. The study compares LLMs of different sizes (LLAMA3-8B, MISTRAL-NEMO-12B, DEEPSEEK-R1-14B, and LLAMA3.3-70B). Key findings include: larger models generally perform better, but strategic prompting can bridge the performance gap; excessive reasoning can negatively impact smaller models on reactive tasks; advanced prompting primarily benefits smaller models on complex games; and advanced reasoning techniques yield variable outcomes, sometimes improving, sometimes degrading performance. The study also points out limitations in LLMs' planning, spatial reasoning, and dynamic task adaptation capabilities. Finally, the authors show that shaping the reward function with more granular information can improve performance, and that current benchmarks need to be re-evaluated to capture true reasoning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Comparison:** The paper provides a systematic comparison of several open-source LLMs across a range of prompting strategies and dynamic environments. This comprehensive approach is valuable for understanding the strengths and weaknesses of different models and techniques.
    *   **Focus on Dynamic Environments:** The emphasis on dynamic environments is a significant strength.  Most LLM evaluations focus on static benchmarks. Evaluating in dynamic settings is crucial for assessing true reasoning and adaptive capabilities, as it exposes limitations in planning, long-term memory, and handling unexpected events.
    *   **Identifies limitations:** The paper is upfront about the limitations of current LLMs, particularly in planning, spatial reasoning, and the stability of advanced prompting techniques. This honesty is valuable for guiding future research.
    *   **Dense Reward Exploration:** The demonstration that shaped, task-aligned reward structures are more effective for LLM learning offers a simple and useful intervention.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Techniques:** The prompting techniques explored (self-reflection, planning) are not entirely novel. While the study systematically examines their application within the SmartPlay environment, the individual techniques themselves are based on established methodologies.
    *   **Variability and Brittle Results:** The variability in performance with advanced prompting raises serious questions about its reliability. The findings suggest that these techniques are not robust and can even degrade performance. More analysis is needed to understand the factors contributing to this instability.
    *   **Reliance on a Specific Benchmark:** While SmartPlay is a reasonable benchmark, the generalizability of the findings to other dynamic environments is uncertain. Using other benchmarks, or creating one's own custom environment would increase the impact of the paper.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    * The use of a multi-faceted study to evaluate LLM reasoning.
    * It highlights crucial shortcomings in areas like planning, spatial coordination, and spatial reasoning when using various reasoning methods.

*   **Justification for Score:**
This paper earns a score of **6**. While the techniques are not groundbreaking, the systematic comparison across various models and dynamic environments is valuable and insightful. It provides an honest view of the limitations of LLMs' reasoning capabilities and highlights the brittleness of current prompting techniques, while underscoring that reward shaping can improve results with less effort.  The focus on dynamic environments sets it apart from many other studies.  The contribution would have been greater with a more novel prompting technique or new benchmark, or, more thorough analysis of why the prompting techniques are so unstable.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Pharmacophore-Conditioned Diffusion Model for Ligand-Based De Novo Drug Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10545v1)
- **Authors**: Amira Alakhdar, Barnabas Poczos, Newell Washburn
- **Abstract**: Developing bioactive molecules remains a central, time- and cost-heavy challenge in drug discovery, particularly for novel targets lacking structural or functional data. Pharmacophore modeling presents an alternative for capturing the key features required for molecular bioactivity against a biological target. In this work, we present PharmaDiff, a pharmacophore-conditioned diffusion model for 3D molecular generation. PharmaDiff employs a transformer-based architecture to integrate an atom-based representation of the 3D pharmacophore into the generative process, enabling the precise generation of 3D molecular graphs that align with predefined pharmacophore hypotheses. Through comprehensive testing, PharmaDiff demonstrates superior performance in matching 3D pharmacophore constraints compared to ligand-based drug design methods. Additionally, it achieves higher docking scores across a range of proteins in structure-based drug design, without the need for target protein structures. By integrating pharmacophore modeling with 3D generative techniques, PharmaDiff offers a powerful and flexible framework for rational drug design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PharmaDiff, a novel deep generative model for *de novo* drug design. PharmaDiff generates 3D molecular structures conditioned on a 3D pharmacophore hypothesis, which represents essential steric and electronic features required for bioactivity against a target. The model uses a transformer-based architecture integrated with a diffusion process to generate molecules that align with predefined pharmacophore constraints. The paper highlights PharmaDiff's superior performance in matching 3D pharmacophore constraints compared to existing ligand-based drug design methods. It also demonstrates higher docking scores across various proteins without requiring protein structure information. The model builds on the MiDi architecture and enhances it with inpainting and cross-attention mechanisms to integrate pharmacophore information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *explicit* conditioning of a 3D diffusion model on a *spatial, atom-based* representation of a 3D pharmacophore hypothesis.  While previous methods have used pharmacophore information (e.g., TransPharmer, LigDream, PGMG), they often use either feature vectors without spatial arrangement or employ SMILES-based generation with graph approximations of 3D structure. PharmaDiff directly generates 3D coordinates while preserving the spatial arrangement of pharmacophoric atoms.  The combination of inpainting with a cross-attention mechanism to fuse the pharmacophore data into the molecular graph generation is also a significant advancement. The use of a transformer-based diffusion model is not entirely novel in molecular generation *per se* (MiDi), but the *pharmacophore-specific adaptation* of this architecture is. The model directly address a limitation of structure-based method such as Pocket2Mol, GraphBP, and DiffSBDD where the 3D structure of target needs to be avaliable to extract the features in order to guide the generation process.

*   **Significance:**  PharmaDiff has the potential to be highly significant for several reasons:

    *   **Target-agnostic *de novo* design:**  It offers a way to generate molecules with specific bioactivity profiles even when structural information about the target protein is unavailable, relying solely on pharmacophore hypotheses. This expands the range of druggable targets.
    *   **Improved Pharmacophore Matching:**  The paper provides strong evidence that PharmaDiff more accurately matches 3D pharmacophore constraints than existing methods, leading to a greater likelihood of generating bioactive molecules.
    *   **Integration of spatial information:** The explicit encoding and utilization of spatial information during generation is a crucial factor for designing molecules that fit the biological target’s binding pocket.
    *   **Enhanced docking scores:**  Achieving higher docking scores compared to other structure-based methods for some targets is promising, implying generated molecules have stronger binding affinity.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-written explanation of the model architecture and methodology.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation including multiple baselines, evaluation metrics (novelty, validity, diversity, docking scores, pharmacophore matching metrics).
    *   Demonstrated superior performance on key metrics related to pharmacophore matching and *de novo* drug design.
    *   Addresses a real-world problem of designing molecules against novel targets or targets where structural data is not available.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The method is dependent on the quality of the pharmacophore hypothesis.  A poorly defined pharmacophore could lead to the generation of irrelevant molecules.  However, this is a limitation inherent to pharmacophore-based methods in general.
    *   Although the paper demonstrated better docking scores than existing methods, it still faces challenges with inpainting process and with the generation of disconnected structures.

*   **Impact:** The paper presents a significant advancement in generative models for drug design by directly incorporating the structural information of a 3D pharmacophore hypothesis. Its potential influence is high due to its target-agnostic nature and improved pharmacophore matching accuracy, making it an attractive tool for generating novel bioactive compounds.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8.  PharmaDiff makes a genuinely novel and significant contribution to *de novo* drug design. Its explicit conditioning on 3D pharmacophore arrangements, resulting in superior pharmacophore matching, addresses a crucial challenge in ligand-based drug discovery. The target-agnostic capability broadens the applicability of generative models. While the method isn't perfect (e.g., potential for disconnected molecules, dependence on pharmacophore quality), it is a significant step forward.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Does Feasibility Matter? Understanding the Impact of Feasibility on Synthetic Training Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10551v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Liu, Jessica Bader, Jae Myung Kim
- **Abstract**: With the development of photorealistic diffusion models, models trained in part or fully on synthetic data achieve progressively better results. However, diffusion models still routinely generate images that would not exist in reality, such as a dog floating above the ground or with unrealistic texture artifacts. We define the concept of feasibility as whether attributes in a synthetic image could realistically exist in the real-world domain; synthetic images containing attributes that violate this criterion are considered infeasible. Intuitively, infeasible images are typically considered out-of-distribution; thus, training on such images is expected to hinder a model's ability to generalize to real-world data, and they should therefore be excluded from the training set whenever possible. However, does feasibility really matter? In this paper, we investigate whether enforcing feasibility is necessary when generating synthetic training data for CLIP-based classifiers, focusing on three target attributes: background, color, and texture. We introduce VariReal, a pipeline that minimally edits a given source image to include feasible or infeasible attributes given by the textual prompt generated by a large language model. Our experiments show that feasibility minimally affects LoRA-fine-tuned CLIP performance, with mostly less than 0.3% difference in top-1 accuracy across three fine-grained datasets. Also, the attribute matters on whether the feasible/infeasible images adversarially influence the classification performance. Finally, mixing feasible and infeasible images in training datasets does not significantly impact performance compared to using purely feasible or infeasible datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Understanding the Impact of Feasibility on Synthetic Training Data":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the question of whether enforcing "feasibility" (i.e., creating images that could plausibly exist in the real world) is necessary when generating synthetic training data, particularly for CLIP-based classifiers. The authors introduce VariReal, a pipeline that minimally edits real images to include either feasible or infeasible attributes across background, color, and texture. They fine-tune CLIP models using synthetic-only and mixed real/synthetic datasets, and then compare performance. The key findings are: 1) feasibility has a minimal impact on CLIP performance, 2) background changes generally improve performance regardless of feasibility, while foreground (color/texture) edits can be detrimental if infeasible, and 3) mixing feasible and infeasible data doesn't significantly impact performance compared to using purely feasible or infeasible data. The authors argue that for object-centric classification, strict feasibility might not be critical.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined and focused research question:** The paper tackles a practical and relevant question in the context of synthetic data generation. Is feasibility important? This question provides a clear goal for the research.
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The paper employs a well-defined experimental methodology. The VariReal pipeline is designed to isolate the effects of feasibility, and the use of CLIP fine-tuning with LoRA provides a robust evaluation framework. The study designs are controlled, comparing feasible, infeasible, mixed, and real-only training scenarios. The paper ablates the generation parameters such as the use of mask-dilation which strengthens the paper.
    *   **Interesting and counter-intuitive findings:**  The finding that strict feasibility isn't always necessary is surprising and challenges common assumptions about synthetic data quality. This highlights the need for a more nuanced understanding of how synthetic data characteristics impact model training.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized, making it relatively easy to follow the experimental design and results.
    *   **Addresses the weaknesses of out-of-the-box methods by combining the individual strengths:** The method addresses the weaknesses of out-of-the-box methods (controlnet, inpainting) and combines the best of both by creating a pipeline.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is focused primarily on CLIP-based classifiers and a limited set of attributes (background, color, texture). The generalizability of these findings to other models, tasks (e.g., object detection, segmentation), and attribute types is unclear. The authors recognize the scope of the study and suggest that the future studies could consider other attributes such as lighting.
    *   **Subjectivity of feasibility:** The definition of "feasibility" relies on human judgement and may be subjective, particularly for fine-grained attributes or edge cases.  While the authors attempt to mitigate this through user studies and automatic filtering, there's still a degree of inherent subjectivity. The use of AI models may result in hallucinated objects as well.
    *   **Choice of Datasets:** The datasets chosen for the study are relatively simple, the choice of waterbirds dataset may impact the results as the background is the most important attribute for that particular problem setting. Exploring performance on datasets with more complex scenes and interactions could provide further insights.

    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** While the paper presents empirical results, it lacks a strong theoretical framework to explain why feasibility doesn't always matter. Developing a theoretical understanding of the underlying mechanisms could strengthen the conclusions.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper makes a novel contribution by systematically investigating the impact of feasibility in synthetic training data. While other works have explored synthetic data generation, this paper uniquely focuses on isolating and analyzing the role of feasibility across different attribute types. The results have practical implications for how synthetic data is generated and used, potentially reducing the need for strict feasibility constraints. The authors present a method which combines different off-the-shelf modules instead of inventing a new method which strengthens the practical applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by challenging common assumptions about synthetic data generation. The experimental design is rigorous, and the findings are interesting and potentially impactful. However, the limited scope of the study, the subjectivity of feasibility, and the lack of theoretical justification hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. Therefore, the score is:

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond 'Aha!': Toward Systematic Meta-Abilities Alignment in Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10554v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan Hu, Yibo Wang, Hanze Dong, Yuhui Xu, Amrita Saha, Caiming Xiong, Bryan Hooi, Junnan Li
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) already possess a latent capacity for long chain-of-thought reasoning. Prior work has shown that outcome-based reinforcement learning (RL) can incidentally elicit advanced reasoning behaviors such as self-correction, backtracking, and verification phenomena often referred to as the model's "aha moment". However, the timing and consistency of these emergent behaviors remain unpredictable and uncontrollable, limiting the scalability and reliability of LRMs' reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we move beyond reliance on prompts and coincidental "aha moments". Instead, we explicitly align models with three meta-abilities: deduction, induction, and abduction, using automatically generated, self-verifiable tasks. Our three stage-pipeline individual alignment, parameter-space merging, and domain-specific reinforcement learning, boosting performance by over 10\% relative to instruction-tuned baselines. Furthermore, domain-specific RL from the aligned checkpoint yields an additional 2\% average gain in the performance ceiling across math, coding, and science benchmarks, demonstrating that explicit meta-ability alignment offers a scalable and dependable foundation for reasoning. Code is available at: https://github.com/zhiyuanhubj/Meta-Ability-Alignment
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond 'Aha!': Toward Systematic Meta-Abilities Alignment in Large Reasoning Models" addresses the issue of unpredictable and uncontrollable emergent reasoning behaviors ("aha moments") in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs). Instead of relying on prompts or coincidence, the authors propose explicitly aligning models with three core meta-abilities: deduction, induction, and abduction. They create an automatically generated, self-verifiable task suite for each meta-ability and implement a three-stage pipeline: (1) individual alignment to each meta-ability, (2) parameter-space merging, and (3) domain-specific reinforcement learning. Their results show significant performance improvements over instruction-tuned baselines and demonstrate that this explicit meta-ability alignment offers a more scalable and dependable foundation for reasoning across math, coding, and science benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its systematic approach to aligning LRMs with foundational reasoning meta-abilities. While prior work has explored emergent reasoning through RL, this work directly targets deduction, induction, and abduction, leveraging classical inference theory.  The creation of the specific task suite for each meta-ability is also novel. Prior approaches focus on emergent reasoning through general reward structures, whereas this creates separate tasks for these foundational skills. The systematic 3-stage approach is innovative and relatively complete.

*   **Significance:** The significance is substantial. The unpredictability of emergent reasoning is a major impediment to deploying LRMs in critical applications. This work tackles that problem directly, providing a way to engineer specific reasoning capabilities. The empirical results support the idea that explicit alignment not only boosts performance but also raises the attainable performance ceiling when combined with domain-specific RL. This aligns with the overall direction in AI toward more controllable behaviors.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of relying solely on emergent reasoning.
    *   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** The choice of deduction, induction, and abduction is well-motivated by inference triad theory.
    *   **Well-Designed Experiments:** The experiments are thorough, comparing against strong baselines and evaluating generalization across multiple benchmarks.
    *   **Scalability Demonstrated:**  The positive results at both 7B and 32B parameter scales suggest the approach is scalable.
    *   **Complete Pipeline:** The methodology (creating meta-ability datasets, combining them, then specializing with RL) is sound and reasonably thorough.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data Dependency:**  The reliance on synthetic data raises questions about real-world applicability.  While the authors claim it's "out-of-distribution" compared to pretraining data, the inherent biases of a synthetic dataset might limit generalization.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The description of hyperparameter tuning is somewhat vague.  The optimal weighting coefficients for merging the models (λd, λi, λa) are mentioned as being "set to" a specific value, with seemingly no justification, opening the door for bias toward the chosen values. Are the results robust to variations in these parameters?
    *   **Limited Ablations:** It would be beneficial to have more ablations to understand the contribution of each stage of the pipeline. For instance, how much does parameter merging contribute compared to just training on a mixed dataset?
    *   **Limited Dataset Exploration:** The paper uses the same dataset as SimpleRL-Zoo [31], which is fine for controlling variables, but it makes it hard to claim the impact of data source, or to determine the best sources of data for meta-alignment. It would be good to see more exploration of data sources.
    *   **No Error Analysis:** Further investigation of the qualitative nature of improvements could strengthen this work. Where specifically does alignment help, and what types of examples are still causing issues?
    *   **Limited Task Diversity within Meta-Abilities:** Though the methodology is presented to be general, each of the meta-abilities is only implemented using one type of task (masked-sequence completion, propositional satisfiability, and reverse-graph rule search). More experiments should be conducted to determine the dependence of the results on task design within each meta-ability.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to shift the focus from simply training massive models and hoping for emergent reasoning to explicitly engineering specific reasoning skills. It could inspire further research into systematic alignment techniques and the development of more reliable and controllable LRMs. The results show strong gains over existing techniques (instruction-tuned models), so it would likely be adopted, especially because the alignment can be bootstrapped with synthetic datasets.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

While the paper has notable strengths, including its novel approach and significant results, the limitations regarding synthetic data dependency and the limited number of tasks tested within each meta-ability hold it back from a higher score. The paper presents a valuable methodology that has a real potential to make LRMs a viable solution in many more areas, but the weaknesses prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The score reflects this balance - a strong and promising contribution with room for significant improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Style Customization of Text-to-Vector Generation with Image Diffusion Priors
